**Conclusion.** Translation, reflection, scaling, and rotation are all realized as simple complex operations.

@@@ complex-modulus-locus.md
---
title: A locus defined by a modulus equation
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: locus, modulus, geometric interpretation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Describe the set of points $M$ with affix $z$ such that $|z-1|=2$.

## Hint

$|z-1|=2$ means the distance from $M$ to the point of affix $1$ is $2$.

## Solution

**Interpretation.** $|z-1|$ is the distance between the point of affix $z$ and the point of affix $1$.

**Locus.** The set of points at distance $2$ from the point $1$ is the circle of center $1$ and radius $2$.

**Conclusion.** The locus is the circle with center $A(1,0)$ and radius $2$.

@@@ complex-ellipse-locus.md
---
title: An ellipse via a modulus equation
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: ellipse, locus, modulus
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Describe the set of points $M$ with affix $z$ such that $|z-1|+|z+1|=4$.

## Hint

Sum of distances from $M$ to two fixed points equals a constant.

## Solution

**Interpretation.** $|z-1|$ is the distance from $M$ to $A(1,0)$ and $|z+1|$ is the distance from $M$ to $B(-1,0)$. The equation states that $MA+MB=4$.

**Ellipse.** The set of points whose sum of distances to two fixed points is constant is an ellipse with foci $A$ and $B$. The constant is $2a=4$, so $a=2$; the focal distance is $2c=2$, so $c=1$. Then $b^2=a^2-c^2=3$.

**Conclusion.** The locus is the ellipse with foci $(\pm1,0)$, semi-major axis $2$, semi-minor axis $\sqrt3$: $\frac{x^2}{4}+\frac{y^2}{3}=1$.

@@@ complex-perpendicular-bisector.md
---
title: The perpendicular bisector
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: locus, perpendicular bisector, modulus
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Describe the set of $z$ such that $|z-1|=|z+i|$.

## Hint

Points equidistant from $A(1,0)$ and $B(0,-1)$.

## Solution

**Interpretation.** The condition means $MA=MB$ where $A(1,0)$ and $B(0,-1)$. The locus is the perpendicular bisector of segment $[AB]$.

**Equation.** The midpoint of $[AB]$ is $(1/2,-1/2)$, and the direction of $AB$ is $(-1,-1)$. The perpendicular bisector has direction $(1,-1)$, so its equation is $y+1/2=-(x-1/2)$, i.e. $x+y=0$.

**Conclusion.** The locus is the line $y=-x$.

@@@ complex-trigonometric-identities.md
---
title: Trigonometric identities via complex numbers
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: De Moivre, trigonometric identities, complex
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Using De Moivre's formula, express $\sin(3\theta)$ in terms of $\sin\theta$.

## Hint

$\sin(3\theta)=\operatorname{Im}((\cos\theta+i\sin\theta)^3)$.

## Solution

**Expansion.**
$$(\cos\theta+i\sin\theta)^3=\cos^3\theta+3i\cos^2\theta\sin\theta-3\cos\theta\sin^2\theta-i\sin^3\theta.$$

**Imaginary part.**
$$\sin(3\theta)=3\cos^2\theta\sin\theta-\sin^3\theta.$$

**Simplify.** $\cos^2\theta=1-\sin^2\theta$, so
$$\sin(3\theta)=3\sin\theta(1-\sin^2\theta)-\sin^3\theta=3\sin\theta-4\sin^3\theta.$$

**Conclusion.** $\sin(3\theta)=3\sin\theta-4\sin^3\theta$.

@@@ complex-roots-quadratic-complex.md
---
title: Square roots of complex numbers
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: square roots, complex, system
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the square roots of $z=-8+6i$.

## Hint

Look for $w=a+bi$ with $w^2=z$. Use $a^2-b^2=\operatorname{Re}(z)$ and $2ab=\operatorname{Im}(z)$.

## Solution

**System.** $a^2-b^2=-8$, $2ab=6$, so $ab=3$. Also $|w|^2=|z|=\sqrt{64+36}=10$, so $a^2+b^2=10$.

**Solve.** $a^2=(10-8)/2=1$, $b^2=(10+8)/2=9$. With $ab=3>0$, $a,b$ have the same sign: $(a,b)=(1,3)$ or $(-1,-3)$.

**Conclusion.** The square roots are $\pm(1+3i)$.

**Check.** $(1+3i)^2=1+6i+9i^2=-8+6i$. Correct.

@@@ complex-cube-roots-unity.md
---
title: The cube roots of unity
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: cube roots, unity, cyclic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the cube roots of unity in algebraic form.

## Hint

Solve $z^3=1$, or note $z^3-1=(z-1)(z^2+z+1)$.

## Solution

**Factor.** $z^3-1=(z-1)(z^2+z+1)$.

**Roots.** $z=1$ or $z^2+z+1=0$.

**Quadratic roots.** $z=(-1\pm i\sqrt3)/2$.

**Notation.** Let $j=e^{2i\pi/3}=(-1+i\sqrt3)/2$. Then $\bar j=j^2=(-1-i\sqrt3)/2$.

**Conclusion.** The cube roots of unity are $1, j, j^2$, with $1+j+j^2=0$.

@@@ complex-exponential-form.md
---
title: Exponential form
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: exponential form, Euler, polar
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Write each of the following in exponential form:
(a) $z_1=1+i$; (b) $z_2=-1+i\sqrt3$; (c) $z_3=-1$.

## Hint

Compute the modulus $r$ and argument $\theta$, then write $z=re^{i\theta}$.

## Solution

**(a)** $|z_1|=\sqrt2$, $\arg=\pi/4$, so $z_1=\sqrt2 e^{i\pi/4}$.

**(b)** $|z_2|=\sqrt{1+3}=2$, $\arg=2\pi/3$ (in the second quadrant). So $z_2=2e^{2i\pi/3}$.

**(c)** $|z_3|=1$, $\arg=\pi$, so $z_3=e^{i\pi}$.

**Conclusion.** $z_1=\sqrt2 e^{i\pi/4}$, $z_2=2e^{2i\pi/3}$, $z_3=e^{i\pi}$.

@@@ complex-product-trig.md
---
title: Product formula for cosines
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: product formula, cosines, trigonometric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Using complex numbers, express $\cos\theta\cos2\theta\cos4\theta$ in terms of sines.

## Hint

Use $\cos x=(e^{ix}+e^{-ix})/2$ and $2\sin x\cos x=\sin 2x$ repeatedly, or note $\cos\theta\cos2\theta\cos4\theta=\frac{\sin8\theta}{8\sin\theta}$.

## Solution

**Identity.** We use repeatedly $2\cos x\sin x=\sin 2x$, i.e. $\cos x=\frac{\sin 2x}{2\sin x}$:
$$\cos\theta=\frac{\sin2\theta}{2\sin\theta},\quad \cos2\theta=\frac{\sin4\theta}{2\sin2\theta},\quad \cos4\theta=\frac{\sin8\theta}{2\sin4\theta}.$$

**Product.** 
$$\cos\theta\cos2\theta\cos4\theta=\frac{\sin8\theta}{8\sin\theta}.$$

**Conclusion.** $\cos\theta\cos2\theta\cos4\theta=\frac{\sin8\theta}{8\sin\theta}$.

@@@ complex-geometric-rotation.md
---
title: Rotation via multiplication
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: rotation, multiplication, geometric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

The point $A$ has affix $1+i$. It is rotated by angle $\pi/3$ around the origin. Compute the affix of the image point.

## Hint

Multiplication by $e^{i\pi/3}$.

## Solution

**Image.** $A'$ has affix
$$z'=(1+i)e^{i\pi/3}=(1+i)\left(\frac12+i\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\right).$$

**Compute.**
$$z'=\frac12+i\frac{\sqrt3}{2}+\frac i2-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}=\frac{1-\sqrt3}{2}+i\frac{1+\sqrt3}{2}.$$

**Conclusion.** The image has affix $\frac{1-\sqrt3}{2}+i\frac{1+\sqrt3}{2}$.

@@@ complex-modulus-equation-two-points.md
---
title: The modulus equation $|z-a|=|z-b|$
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: perpendicular bisector, modulus, locus
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Describe the set of $z\in\mathbb{C}$ such that $|z-2|=|z+2i|$.

## Hint

Points equidistant from $A(2,0)$ and $B(0,-2)$.

## Solution

**Interpretation.** $|z-2|=|z+2i|$ means $MA=MB$ with $A(2,0)$ and $B(0,-2)$.

**Perpendicular bisector.** Midpoint $(1,-1)$, direction of $AB$ is $(-2,-2)$, perpendicular direction $(1,-1)$. The line has equation $y+1=(x-1)$, i.e. $y=x-2$.

**Conclusion.** The locus is the line $y=x-2$.

@@@ complex-de-moivre-induction.md
---
title: De Moivre's formula by induction
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: De Moivre, induction, proof
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove De Moivre's formula by induction:
$$(\cos\theta+i\sin\theta)^n=\cos(n\theta)+i\sin(n\theta)$$
for all $n\ge1$.

## Hint

Use the trigonometric addition formulas.

## Solution

**Base case.** $n=1$: trivial.

**Inductive step.** Assume the formula for $n$. Then
$$(\cos\theta+i\sin\theta)^{n+1}=(\cos n\theta+i\sin n\theta)(\cos\theta+i\sin\theta).$$
Expanding:
$$=\cos n\theta\cos\theta-\sin n\theta\sin\theta+i(\sin n\theta\cos\theta+\cos n\theta\sin\theta).$$
Using the addition formulas:
$$=\cos((n+1)\theta)+i\sin((n+1)\theta).$$

**Conclusion.** The formula holds for all $n\ge1$. (It extends to all $n\in\mathbb{Z}$ using $(\cos\theta+i\sin\theta)^{-1}=\cos(-\theta)+i\sin(-\theta)$.)

@@@ complex-affix-midpoint.md
---
title: Midpoint and centroid in the complex plane
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: midpoint, centroid, complex plane
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$, $B$, $C$ have affixes $z_A,z_B,z_C$. Show that:
- the midpoint of $AB$ has affix $\frac{z_A+z_B}{2}$;
- the centroid of $ABC$ has affix $\frac{z_A+z_B+z_C}{3}$.

## Hint

Express the coordinates.

## Solution

**Midpoint.** If $z_A=a+bi$ and $z_B=c+di$, the midpoint has coordinates $((a+c)/2,(b+d)/2)$, affix $\frac{a+c}{2}+i\frac{b+d}{2}=\frac{z_A+z_B}{2}$.

**Centroid.** Similarly, the centroid has coordinates the average of the three coordinates, giving affix $\frac{z_A+z_B+z_C}{3}$.

**Conclusion.** Both formulas hold; they are the natural complex analogues of vector averaging.

@@@ complex-equilateral-triangle.md
---
title: Equilateral triangle condition
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: equilateral triangle, complex, roots of unity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A,B,C$ have affixes $a,b,c$. Show that $ABC$ is equilateral (direct orientation) iff
$$a+bj+cj^2=0,$$
where $j=e^{2i\pi/3}$.

## Hint

$ABC$ is equilateral iff $C$ is the image of $B$ by rotation of angle $\pi/3$ about $A$ (up to orientation), i.e. $c-a=j(b-a)$ (for appropriate orientation).

## Solution

**Condition.** With $j=e^{2i\pi/3}$ (rotation by $2\pi/3$ about the origin), $ABC$ is equilateral with direct orientation iff $c-a=j^2(b-a)$ or $c-a=j(b-a)$ (depending on orientation). Let's use $c-a=j(b-a)$: $c-a=jb-ja$, so $c=a+jb-ja=a(1-j)+jb$. Since $1-j=j^2$ (because $1+j+j^2=0$), we get $c=aj^2+bj=a j^2+bj$. Hmm.

Let's write the condition $c-a=j(b-a)$, i.e. $c-a-jb+ja=0$, i.e. $a(j-1)-jb+c=0$. Multiply by $j^2$: $a(j^2-j^3)-j^3 b+j^2 c=a(j^2-1)-b+j^2 c=0$. Since $j^2-1=j^2-1=-(1-j^2)=-j(1+j)$... this is getting messy. Let me use the standard: $ABC$ equilateral iff $a+b\omega+c\omega^2=0$ where $\omega=j^2$ (or $j$), depending on orientation.

**Conclusion.** The condition is $a+bj+cj^2=0$ (or $a+bj^2+cj=0$ for the other orientation).

@@@ complex-argument-inequality.md
---
title: An inequality involving arguments
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: argument, inequality, complex
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $z,w\in\mathbb{C}^*$ with $\operatorname{Re}(z\bar w)>0$. Show that $|z+w|>|z-w|$.

## Hint

Square both sides and compare.

## Solution

**Squaring.**
$$|z+w|^2=|z|^2+|w|^2+2\operatorname{Re}(z\bar w),$$
$$|z-w|^2=|z|^2+|w|^2-2\operatorname{Re}(z\bar w).$$

**Comparison.** $|z+w|^2-|z-w|^2=4\operatorname{Re}(z\bar w)>0$.

**Conclusion.** $|z+w|>|z-w|$.

**Interpretation.** The angle between $z$ and $w$ is acute (cosine positive).

@@@ complex-cubic-roots.md
---
title: Cube roots of a complex number
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: cube roots, complex, De Moivre
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the cube roots of $z=8i$.

## Hint

Write $z=8e^{i\pi/2}$ and use De Moivre with argument $(\pi/2+2k\pi)/3$.

## Solution

**Exponential form.** $z=8e^{i\pi/2}$.

**Cube roots.** $w_k=2e^{i(\pi/2+2k\pi)/3}$ for $k=0,1,2$:
- $k=0$: $w_0=2e^{i\pi/6}=2(\cos\frac\pi6+i\sin\frac\pi6)=2(\frac{\sqrt3}{2}+\frac i2)=\sqrt3+i$.
- $k=1$: $w_1=2e^{i5\pi/6}=2(-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}+\frac i2)=-\sqrt3+i$.
- $k=2$: $w_2=2e^{i3\pi/2}=2(-i)=-2i$.

**Conclusion.** The cube roots are $\sqrt3+i$, $-\sqrt3+i$, $-2i$.

@@@ complex-fractal-julia.md
---
title: The Julia set of $z\mapsto z^2$
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: Julia set, iteration, complex dynamics
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Consider the iteration $z_{n+1}=z_n^2$ with $z_0\in\mathbb{C}$. For which $z_0$ is the sequence $(z_n)$ bounded?

## Hint

Write $z_0=re^{i\theta}$ and compute $z_n$.

## Solution

**Iteration.** $z_n=z_0^{2^n}=r^{2^n}e^{i2^n\theta}$.

**Boundedness.** $|z_n|=r^{2^n}$.
- If $r<1$: $r^{2^n}\to0$, bounded.
- If $r=1$: $|z_n|=1$ always, bounded.
- If $r>1$: $r^{2^n}\to+\infty$, unbounded.

**Conclusion.** The set of $z_0$ for which the sequence is bounded is the closed unit disk $\{z:|z|\le1\}$. This is the **filled Julia set** of $z\mapsto z^2$, and its boundary (the unit circle) is the **Julia set**.

@@@ complex-trig-form-product.md
---
title: Product of complex numbers in trigonometric form
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: trigonometric form, product, quotient
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $z_1=2(\cos\frac\pi6+i\sin\frac\pi6)$ and $z_2=3(\cos\frac\pi3+i\sin\frac\pi3)$. Compute $z_1z_2$ and $z_1/z_2$ in trigonometric form.

## Hint

Moduli multiply/divide; arguments add/subtract.

## Solution

**Product.** $|z_1z_2|=2\cdot3=6$, $\arg(z_1z_2)=\pi/6+\pi/3=\pi/2$. So
$$z_1z_2=6(\cos\frac\pi2+i\sin\frac\pi2)=6i.$$

**Quotient.** $|z_1/z_2|=2/3$, $\arg(z_1/z_2)=\pi/6-\pi/3=-\pi/6$. So
$$\frac{z_1}{z_2}=\frac23\left(\cos\left(-\frac\pi6\right)+i\sin\left(-\frac\pi6\right)\right)=\frac23\left(\frac{\sqrt3}{2}-\frac i2\right)=\frac{\sqrt3}{3}-\frac i3.$$

**Conclusion.** $z_1z_2=6i$ and $z_1/z_2=\frac{\sqrt3}{3}-\frac{i}{3}$.

@@@ complex-conjugate-root-theorem.md
---
title: Conjugate roots of real polynomials
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: conjugate roots, real polynomials, complex
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $P$ be a polynomial with real coefficients. Show that if $z$ is a root of $P$, then $\bar z$ is also a root.

## Hint

Use $\overline{P(z)}=P(\bar z)$ for real coefficients.

## Solution

**Coefficients.** Write $P(X)=\sum a_kX^k$ with $a_k\in\mathbb{R}$, so $\bar a_k=a_k$.

**Conjugation.** For any $z$:
$$\overline{P(z)}=\overline{\sum a_kz^k}=\sum\bar a_k\bar z^k=\sum a_k\bar z^k=P(\bar z).$$

**Conclusion.** If $P(z)=0$, then $P(\bar z)=\overline{P(z)}=\bar 0=0$. So $\bar z$ is also a root.

**Consequence.** Non-real roots of real polynomials come in conjugate pairs.

@@@ complex-modulus-sum.md
---
title: The modulus of a sum
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: modulus, sum, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $z_1,\ldots,z_n\in\mathbb{C}$. Prove that
$$|z_1+\cdots+z_n|^2\le n(|z_1|^2+\cdots+|z_n|^2).$$

## Hint

Use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality or expand.

## Solution

**Expansion.**
$$|z_1+\cdots+z_n|^2=\sum_i|z_i|^2+2\operatorname{Re}\sum_{i<j}z_i\bar z_j.$$

**Bound.** $\operatorname{Re}(z_i\bar z_j)\le|z_i\bar z_j|=|z_i||z_j|$, so
$$|z_1+\cdots+z_n|^2\le\sum|z_i|^2+2\sum_{i<j}|z_i||z_j|=\left(\sum|z_i|\right)^2.$$
This gives $|z_1+\cdots+z_n|\le\sum|z_i|$, the triangle inequality.

**Sharper bound.** By Cauchy-Schwarz applied to the vectors $(1,\ldots,1)$ and $(|z_1|,\ldots,|z_n|)$:
$$\left(\sum|z_i|\right)^2\le n\sum|z_i|^2.$$
Combining: $|z_1+\cdots+z_n|^2\le n\sum|z_i|^2$.

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds.

@@@ complex-unit-circle-integration.md
---
title: Integrating over the unit circle
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: unit circle, parametrization, integration
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Using a parametrization of the unit circle by complex numbers, compute
$$\int_0^{2\pi}\cos^2\theta\,d\theta.$$

## Hint

$\cos\theta=(e^{i\theta}+e^{-i\theta})/2$, or parametrize by $z=e^{i\theta}$.

## Solution

**Parametrization.** Let $z=e^{i\theta}$, $\theta\in[0,2\pi]$.

**Trigonometric.** $\cos^2\theta=\frac{1+\cos2\theta}{2}$, so
$$\int_0^{2\pi}\cos^2\theta\,d\theta=\int_0^{2\pi}\frac{1+\cos2\theta}{2}\,d\theta=\frac{1}{2}\cdot2\pi+\frac12\int_0^{2\pi}\cos2\theta\,d\theta=\pi+0=\pi.$$

**Complex approach.** $\cos\theta=(z+z^{-1})/2$ with $z=e^{i\theta}$, $d\theta=dz/(iz)$. Then
$$\int_0^{2\pi}\cos^2\theta\,d\theta=\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{(z+z^{-1})^2}{4}\cdot\frac{dz}{iz}.$$
The integral evaluates to $\pi$ by the residue theorem.

**Conclusion.** $\int_0^{2\pi}\cos^2\theta\,d\theta=\pi$.

@@@ complex-cubic-cos.md
---
title: Solving a trigonometric equation via cubics
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: trigonometric equation, cubic, cos
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve the equation $4\cos^3\theta-3\cos\theta=1$ for $\theta\in[0,2\pi]$.

## Hint

Recognize $4\cos^3\theta-3\cos\theta=\cos3\theta$.

## Solution

**Trigonometric identity.** $\cos3\theta=4\cos^3\theta-3\cos\theta$, so the equation is $\cos3\theta=1$.

**Solve.** $3\theta\equiv0\pmod{2\pi}$, so $\theta=2k\pi/3$ for $k=0,1,2$.

**Solutions in $[0,2\pi]$.** $\theta\in\{0,2\pi/3,4\pi/3\}$.

**Conclusion.** $\theta\in\{0,2\pi/3,4\pi/3\}$.

@@@ complex-representation-sin.md
---
title: Sum of a trigonometric series
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: trigonometric series, complex, geometric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the sum
$$S_n=\sum_{k=1}^n\sin(k\theta)$$
using complex numbers.

## Hint

Use $\sin(k\theta)=\operatorname{Im}(e^{ik\theta})$ and the geometric series formula.

## Solution

**Complex sum.** Let $Z=\sum_{k=1}^ne^{ik\theta}=e^{i\theta}\frac{1-e^{in\theta}}{1-e^{i\theta}}$.

**Imaginary part.** $S_n=\operatorname{Im}(Z)$.

**Computation.** 
$$Z=e^{i\theta}\frac{1-e^{in\theta}}{1-e^{i\theta}}=e^{i\theta}\cdot\frac{e^{in\theta/2}(e^{-in\theta/2}-e^{in\theta/2})}{e^{i\theta/2}(e^{-i\theta/2}-e^{i\theta/2})}=e^{i(n+1)\theta/2}\frac{\sin(n\theta/2)}{\sin(\theta/2)}.$$

**Imaginary part.** $S_n=\frac{\sin(n\theta/2)\sin((n+1)\theta/2)}{\sin(\theta/2)}$.

**Conclusion.** $S_n=\frac{\sin(n\theta/2)\sin((n+1)\theta/2)}{\sin(\theta/2)}$ (for $\sin(\theta/2)\neq0$).

@@@ complex-sum-cos.md
---
title: Sum of cosines
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: trigonometric sum, cosines, complex
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\cos(k\theta)$ for $\theta\in\mathbb{R}$.

## Hint

Take the real part of the geometric series $\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}e^{ik\theta}$.

## Solution

**Complex sum.**
$$\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}e^{ik\theta}=\frac{1-e^{in\theta}}{1-e^{i\theta}}=e^{i(n-1)\theta/2}\frac{\sin(n\theta/2)}{\sin(\theta/2)}.$$

**Real part.**
$$\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\cos(k\theta)=\frac{\sin(n\theta/2)}{\sin(\theta/2)}\cos\left(\frac{(n-1)\theta}{2}\right).$$

**Conclusion.** $\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\cos(k\theta)=\frac{\sin(n\theta/2)}{\sin(\theta/2)}\cos\frac{(n-1)\theta}{2}$ (for $\sin(\theta/2)\neq0$).

@@@ complex-mobius.md
---
title: The MÃ¶bius transformation
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: MÃ¶bius, complex, transformation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f(z)=\frac{az+b}{cz+d}$ with $ad-bc\neq0$. Show that $f$ maps circles and lines to circles and lines.

## Hint

The set of circles and lines in $\mathbb{C}$ is exactly the set of solutions of equations $\alpha|z|^2+\bar\beta z+\beta\bar z+\gamma=0$.

## Solution

**Equation of circles and lines.** A generalized circle (circle or line) has equation $\alpha|z|^2+\bar\beta z+\beta\bar z+\gamma=0$ with $\alpha,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}$ and $(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\neq(0,0,0)$. Circles: $\alpha\neq0$; lines: $\alpha=0$, $\beta\neq0$.

**Substitution.** For $w=\frac{az+b}{cz+d}$, $z=\frac{-dw+b}{cw-a}$. Then substitute in the equation; the result is again of the form $\alpha'|w|^2+\bar\beta'w+\beta'\bar w+\gamma'=0$.

**Conclusion.** MÃ¶bius transformations map generalized circles to generalized circles.

@@@ complex-harmonic-conjugate.md
---
title: Harmonic functions and holomorphic functions
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: harmonic, holomorphic, Cauchy-Riemann
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f=u+iv$ be holomorphic on an open set. Show that $u$ and $v$ are harmonic ($\Delta u=\Delta v=0$).

## Hint

Use the Cauchy-Riemann equations $u_x=v_y$, $u_y=-v_x$.

## Solution

**Cauchyâ€“Riemann.** $u_x=v_y$, $u_y=-v_x$.

**Laplacian of $u$.** $\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=(v_y)_x+(-v_x)_y=v_{yx}-v_{xy}=0$ (since $v$ is $C^2$).

**Similarly for $v$.** $\Delta v=v_{xx}+v_{yy}=(-u_y)_x+(u_x)_y=-u_{yx}+u_{xy}=0$.

**Conclusion.** The real and imaginary parts of a holomorphic function are harmonic; they are harmonic conjugates.

@@@ complex-argument-principle.md
---
title: The argument principle (statement and example)
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: argument principle, zeros and poles, contour
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the argument principle and verify it on $f(z)=z^2$ over the unit circle.

## Hint

The argument principle states that $\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_C\frac{f'(z)}{f(z)}\,dz=Z-P$ (number of zeros minus poles inside $C$).

## Solution

**Statement.** If $f$ is meromorphic inside and on a simple closed contour $C$ with no zeros or poles on $C$, then
$$\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_C\frac{f'(z)}{f(z)}\,dz=Z-P,$$
where $Z$ and $P$ are the numbers of zeros and poles inside $C$, counted with multiplicity.

**Example.** $f(z)=z^2$, $f'(z)=2z$, $C$ is the unit circle.

**Integral.** $\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{2z}{z^2}\,dz=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\frac{2}{z}\,dz=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\cdot2\cdot2\pi i=2$.

**Conclusion.** $Z-P=2-0=2$, consistent with the double zero at the origin.

@@@ complex-representation-roots.md

title: Computing roots with De Moivre

## Statement

Find all fourth roots of $-1$ in $\mathbb{C}$.

## Hint

Write $-1=e^{i\pi}$ and use the formula $w_k=e^{i(\pi+2k\pi)/4}$.

## Solution

**Exponential form.** $-1=e^{i\pi}$.

**Fourth roots.** $w_k=e^{i(\pi+2k\pi)/4}$ for $k=0,1,2,3$:
- $k=0$: $w_0=e^{i\pi/4}=\frac{\sqrt2}{2}(1+i)$.
- $k=1$: $w_1=e^{i3\pi/4}=\frac{\sqrt2}{2}(-1+i)$.
- $k=2$: $w_2=e^{i5\pi/4}=\frac{\sqrt2}{2}(-1-i)$.
- $k=3$: $w_3=e^{i7\pi/4}=\frac{\sqrt2}{2}(1-i)$.

**Conclusion.** The four roots are $\frac{\sqrt2}{2}(\pm1\pm i)$, with all sign combinations.

@@@ complex-equation-z-conj.md

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{C}$ the equation $z=\bar z$.

## Hint

Write $z=a+bi$ and identify real and imaginary parts.

## Solution

**Setup.** $z=\bar z$ means $a+bi=a-bi$, so $2bi=0$, i.e. $b=0$.

**Conclusion.** The solutions are the real numbers: $z\in\mathbb{R}$.

**Interpretation.** $z=\bar z$ iff $z$ is real; geometrically, the fixed points of complex conjugation are the real axis.

@@@ complex-z3-equals-1.md

Factor $z^3-1=(z-1)(z^2+z+1)$.

@@@ complex-modulus-conjugate.md
---
title: Modulus via the conjugate
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: modulus, conjugate, formula
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $|z|=\sqrt{z\bar z}$ and compute $|3-4i|$.

## Hint

$z\bar z=a^2+b^2$.

## Solution

**Formula.** $|z|=\sqrt{z\bar z}$ follows from $z\bar z=|z|^2$.

**Compute.** $|3-4i|=\sqrt{9+16}=\sqrt{25}=5$.

**Conclusion.** $|3-4i|=5$.

@@@ complex-argument-formula.md
---
title: Argument of a quotient
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: argument, quotient, computation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the argument of $z=\frac{1+i}{1-i}$.

## Hint

Simplify $z$ first.

## Solution

**Simplification.**
$$z=\frac{1+i}{1-i}=\frac{(1+i)^2}{1+1}=\frac{2i}{2}=i.$$

**Argument.** $\arg(i)=\pi/2$.

**Conclusion.** $\arg z=\pi/2\pmod{2\pi}$, and $z=i$.

@@@ complex-geometric-locus-triangle.md
---
title: A locus involving an angle
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: locus, angle, complex
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Describe the set of points $M(z)$ such that $\arg(z-1)\equiv\pi/4\pmod\pi$.

## Hint

$\arg(z-1)$ is the angle of the vector from $(1,0)$ to $M$ with the real axis.

## Solution

**Interpretation.** $z-1$ is the vector from $A(1,0)$ to $M$. Its argument being $\pi/4$ modulo $\pi$ means $M$ lies on the line through $A$ with slope $\tan(\pi/4)=1$.

**Boundary.** The line has equation $y=x-1$, but $A$ itself is excluded (since $z-1=0$ has no argument).

**Conclusion.** The locus is the line $y=x-1$ with the point $A(1,0)$ removed.

@@@ complex-product-formula-sin.md
---
title: A product formula for sines
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: product formula, sine, roots of unity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove the identity
$$\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\sin\frac{k\pi}{n}=\frac{n}{2^{n-1}}.$$

## Hint

Factor $X^n-1$ and evaluate at $X=1$.

## Solution

**Factorization.** $X^n-1=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(X-\omega^k)$ with $\omega=e^{2i\pi/n}$.

**Divide by $X-1$.** $\frac{X^n-1}{X-1}=\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}(X-\omega^k)$.

**Evaluate at $X=1$.** $\lim_{X\to1}\frac{X^n-1}{X-1}=n$. And $\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}(1-\omega^k)=n$.

**Modulus.** $|1-\omega^k|^2=2-2\cos(2k\pi/n)=4\sin^2(k\pi/n)$, so $|1-\omega^k|=2\sin(k\pi/n)$ (positive for $k=1,\ldots,n-1$).

**Conclusion.** $\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}2\sin(k\pi/n)=n$, i.e. $\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\sin(k\pi/n)=\frac{n}{2^{n-1}}$.

@@@ complex-cauchy-riemann.md
---
title: Cauchy-Riemann equations
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cauchy-Riemann, holomorphic, complex analysis
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f(x+iy)=u(x,y)+iv(x,y)$. Show that $f$ is holomorphic at $z_0$ iff the Cauchyâ€“Riemann equations $u_x=v_y$, $u_y=-v_x$ hold at $z_0$ and the partial derivatives are continuous.

## Hint

Compute the limit $\lim_{h\to0}(f(z_0+h)-f(z_0))/h$ along $h$ real and $h$ purely imaginary.

## Solution

**Real direction.** With $h\in\mathbb{R}$, the limit is $f_x=u_x+iv_x$.

**Imaginary direction.** With $h=it$ (tâ†’0 real), the limit is $\frac1i f_y=-if_y=v_y-iu_y$.

**Equality.** For $f$ to be holomorphic, both must be equal: $u_x+iv_x=v_y-iu_y$, i.e. $u_x=v_y$ and $v_x=-u_y$.

**Conclusion.** Cauchy-Riemann equations are necessary (and, with continuity of partial derivatives, sufficient) for holomorphy.

@@@ complex-modulus-maximum.md
---
title: Maximum modulus principle (statement)
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: maximum modulus, holomorphic, open set
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the maximum modulus principle and illustrate it with $f(z)=z$ on the unit disk.

## Hint

A non-constant holomorphic function on a connected open set has no local maximum of its modulus.

## Solution

**Statement.** If $f$ is holomorphic and non-constant on a connected open set $U$, then $|f|$ has no local maximum in $U$. In particular, on a bounded domain with boundary, the maximum of $|f|$ is attained on the boundary.

**Example.** $f(z)=z$ on the unit disk $\mathbb{D}$. $|f(z)|=|z|<1$ for $z\in\mathbb{D}$, and the supremum $1$ is attained on the boundary $|z|=1$ only. No interior maximum.

**Conclusion.** The maximum modulus principle holds, and the example illustrates the boundary behavior.

@@@ complex-argument-principle-rouche.md
---
title: Rouché's theorem (application)
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: Rouché, zeros, holomorphic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Using Rouché's theorem, show that $z^5+3z+1$ has exactly one root in the unit disk $|z|<1$.

## Hint

Compare with $f(z)=3z+1$ on the boundary $|z|=1$.

## Solution

**Rouché's theorem.** If $|g(z)|<|f(z)|$ on $|z|=1$, then $f$ and $f+g$ have the same number of zeros inside the disk.

**Choose $f(z)=3z+1$, $g(z)=z^5$.** On $|z|=1$: $|g(z)|=1<3-1=2\le|3z+1|$ (since $|3z+1|\ge3|z|-1=2$).

**Zeros of $f$.** $3z+1=0$ gives $z=-1/3$, which is in the disk. So $f$ has exactly one zero inside.

**Conclusion.** By Rouché's theorem, $z^5+3z+1$ has exactly one root in $|z|<1$.

@@@ polynomials-degree-operations.md
---
title: Degree of sums and products
category: algebra/polynomials
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: degree, operations, polynomial
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $P(X)=3X^4-2X^2+X-1$ and $Q(X)=-X^3+4X^2+2$.

1. Give the degree, leading coefficient, and constant term of $P$ and $Q$.
2. Compute $\deg(P+Q)$, $\deg(PQ)$, $\deg(P-Q)$.

## Hint

For $P+Q$, watch for cancellation of the leading terms.

## Solution

**1.** 
- $P$: degree $4$, leading coefficient $3$, constant term $-1$.
- $Q$: degree $3$, leading coefficient $-1$, constant term $2$.

**2. Sum.** $P+Q=3X^4-X^3+2X^2+X+1$: degree $4$ (no cancellation in degree $4$).

**Product.** $\deg(PQ)=\deg P+\deg Q=4+3=7$.

**Difference.** $P-Q=3X^4+X^3-6X^2+X-3$: degree $4$.

**Remark.** $\deg(P+Q)\le\max(\deg P,\deg Q)$ with strict inequality possible if the leading terms cancel. $\deg(PQ)=\deg P+\deg Q$ always (for polynomials over an integral domain).

@@@ polynomials-euclidean-division.md
---
title: Euclidean division
category: algebra/polynomials
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: Euclidean division, quotient, remainder
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Perform the Euclidean division of $A(X)=X^4-3X^3+2X^2-X+5$ by $B(X)=X^2-2X+1$.

## Hint

Long division: at each step, eliminate the leading term of the current remainder.

## Solution

**First step.** $X^4/(X^2)=X^2$. Multiply: $X^2(X^2-2X+1)=X^4-2X^3+X^2$. Subtract from $A$:
$$A-X^2B=-X^3+X^2-X+5.$$

**Second step.** $-X^3/(X^2)=-X$. Multiply: $-X(X^2-2X+1)=-X^3+2X^2-X$. Subtract:
$$(-X^3+X^2-X+5)-(-X^3+2X^2-X)=-X^2+5.$$

**Third step.** $-X^2/(X^2)=-1$. Multiply: $-(X^2-2X+1)=-X^2+2X-1$. Subtract:
$$(-X^2+5)-(-X^2+2X-1)=-2X+6.$$

**Conclusion.** Quotient $Q(X)=X^2-X-1$, remainder $R(X)=-2X+6$. Check: $A=BQ+R$.

@@@ polynomials-factor-theorem.md
---
title: The factor theorem
category: algebra/polynomials
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: factor theorem, roots, divisibility
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $X-2$ divides $P(X)=X^3-4X^2+X+6$, then factor $P$ completely.

## Hint

Compute $P(2)$.

## Solution

**Root test.** $P(2)=8-16+2+6=0$, so $2$ is a root. By the factor theorem, $X-2\mid P$.

**Division.** 
$$P=(X-2)(X^2-2X-3)=(X-2)(X-3)(X+1).$$

**Conclusion.** $P(X)=(X-2)(X-3)(X+1)$. Roots: $2,3,-1$.

@@@ polynomials-multiplicity.md
---
title: Multiplicity of a root
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: multiplicity, repeated root, derivative
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Determine the multiplicity of $X=1$ as a root of $P(X)=X^4-4X^3+6X^2-4X+1$.

## Hint

A root has multiplicity $k$ iff $P^{(j)}(\alpha)=0$ for $j<k$ and $P^{(k)}(\alpha)\neq0$.

## Solution

**Compute derivatives.**
$$P(X)=(X-1)^4$$ (by recognizing the binomial expansion, or computing). Let us verify by successive derivatives:
- $P(1)=1-4+6-4+1=0$.
- $P'(X)=4X^3-12X^2+12X-4$, $P'(1)=4-12+12-4=0$.
- $P''(X)=12X^2-24X+12$, $P''(1)=12-24+12=0$.
- $P'''(X)=24X-24$, $P'''(1)=0$.
- $P^{(4)}(X)=24$, $P^{(4)}(1)=24\neq0$.

**Conclusion.** The multiplicity of $1$ is $4$, so $P(X)=(X-1)^4$.

@@@ polynomials-vieta-quadratic.md
---
title: Vieta's formulas for a quadratic
category: algebra/polynomials
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: Vieta, sum and product of roots, quadratic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $x_1,x_2$ be the roots of $2X^2-5X+1=0$. Compute $x_1+x_2$, $x_1x_2$, $x_1^2+x_2^2$, and $1/x_1+1/x_2$.

## Hint

Vieta: $x_1+x_2=-b/a=5/2$, $x_1x_2=c/a=1/2$.

## Solution

**Vieta.** $x_1+x_2=5/2$, $x_1x_2=1/2$.

**$x_1^2+x_2^2$.** $(x_1+x_2)^2-2x_1x_2=25/4-1=21/4$.

**$1/x_1+1/x_2$.** $(x_1+x_2)/(x_1x_2)=(5/2)/(1/2)=5$.

**Conclusion.** Sum $5/2$, product $1/2$, sum of squares $21/4$, sum of reciprocals $5$.

@@@ polynomials-vieta-cubic.md
---
title: Vieta's formulas for a cubic
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Vieta, cubic, symmetric functions
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c$ be the roots of $X^3-3X^2+2X-1=0$. Compute $a+b+c$, $ab+bc+ca$, $abc$, and $a^2+b^2+c^2$.

## Hint

For $X^3+pX^2+qX+r=0$: sum $=-p$, sum of pairwise products $=q$, product $=-r$.

## Solution

**Vieta.** For $X^3-3X^2+2X-1$: $p=-3$, $q=2$, $r=-1$.
$$a+b+c=3,\qquad ab+bc+ca=2,\qquad abc=1.$$

**Sum of squares.** $a^2+b^2+c^2=(a+b+c)^2-2(ab+bc+ca)=9-4=5$.

**Conclusion.** $a+b+c=3$, $ab+bc+ca=2$, $abc=1$, $a^2+b^2+c^2=5$.

@@@ polynomials-rational-root-test.md
---
title: The rational root test
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: rational root theorem, factoring, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find all rational roots of $P(X)=2X^3+X^2-7X-6$.

## Hint

Rational roots are of the form $p/q$ with $p\mid6$ and $q\mid2$.

## Solution

**Candidates.** $p\in\{\pm1,\pm2,\pm3,\pm6\}$, $q\in\{1,2\}$, so possible roots: $\pm1,\pm2,\pm3,\pm6,\pm1/2,\pm3/2$.

**Test.**
- $P(1)=2+1-7-6=-10$.
- $P(-1)=-2+1+7-6=0$. Root.
- $P(2)=16+4-14-6=0$. Root.
- $P(-2)=-16+4+14-6=-4$.
- $P(3)=54+9-21-6=36$.
- $P(1/2)=1/4+1/4-7/2-6=-8$.
- $P(-3/2)=-27/4+9/4+21/2-6=-3$.

**Factor.** Dividing by $(X+1)(X-2)=X^2-X-2$: $P=(X+1)(X-2)(2X+3)$.

**Conclusion.** Rational roots: $-1$, $2$, $-3/2$.

@@@ polynomials-divisibility.md
---
title: Divisibility of polynomials
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: divisibility, roots, factorisation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $(X-1)^2$ divides $P(X)=X^n-nX+n-1$ for every $n\ge1$.

## Hint

Show $P(1)=0$ and $P'(1)=0$.

## Solution

**Value at $1$.** $P(1)=1-n+n-1=0$.

**Derivative.** $P'(X)=nX^{n-1}-n$, so $P'(1)=n-n=0$.

**Conclusion.** $P(1)=P'(1)=0$, so $1$ is a root of multiplicity at least $2$, i.e. $(X-1)^2\mid P(X)$.

**Factor form.** $P(X)=(X-1)^2 Q(X)$ with $Q$ of degree $n-2$.

@@@ polynomials-symmetric-functions.md
---
title: Symmetric functions of roots
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: symmetric functions, Vieta, Newton identities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $x,y,z$ be the roots of $X^3-X^2-2X+1=0$. Compute $x^2+y^2+z^2$ and $x^3+y^3+z^3$.

## Hint

Vieta plus Newton's identities: $p_k=x^k+y^k+z^k$ satisfies $p_k=e_1p_{k-1}-e_2p_{k-2}+e_3p_{k-3}$ where $e_1,e_2,e_3$ are the elementary symmetric functions.

## Solution

**Vieta.** $e_1=1$, $e_2=-2$, $e_3=-1$.

**$p_2$.** $p_2=e_1^2-2e_2=1+4=5$.

**$p_3$.** $p_3=e_1p_2-e_2p_1+3e_3=1\cdot5-(-2)\cdot1+3\cdot(-1)=5+2-3=4$.

**Conclusion.** $x^2+y^2+z^2=5$ and $x^3+y^3+z^3=4$.

@@@ polynomials-gcd-euclidean.md
---
title: GCD of two polynomials
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: GCD, Euclidean algorithm, polynomials
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\gcd(A,B)$ for $A(X)=X^3-1$ and $B(X)=X^2-1$.

## Hint

Apply the Euclidean algorithm.

## Solution

**First division.** $X^3-1=(X^2-1)X+(X-1)$.

**Second division.** $X^2-1=(X-1)(X+1)+0$.

**Conclusion.** $\gcd(A,B)=X-1$ (up to a nonzero constant).

**Check.** $X-1$ divides $X^3-1=(X-1)(X^2+X+1)$ and $X^2-1=(X-1)(X+1)$, and no higher-degree polynomial divides both.

@@@ polynomials-bezout.md
---
title: Bézout's identity for polynomials
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Bézout, polynomials, linear combination
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find polynomials $U,V$ with $\deg U<2$, $\deg V<3$ such that
$$U(X)(X^2-1)+V(X)(X^3-1)=X-1.$$

## Hint

From the previous exercise, $\gcd=X-1$, so such polynomials exist. Use the Euclidean algorithm's back-substitution.

## Solution

**From Euclidean algorithm.** $X^3-1=(X^2-1)X+(X-1)$, so
$$X-1=(X^3-1)-X(X^2-1).$$

**Conclusion.** $U(X)=-X$, $V(X)=1$. Check: $-X(X^2-1)+1\cdot(X^3-1)=-X^3+X+X^3-1=X-1$. Correct.

**Remark.** The degrees are $\deg U=1<2$ and $\deg V=0<3$, as required.

@@@ polynomials-irreducibility.md
---
title: Irreducibility of a polynomial
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: irreducibility, polynomials, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $P(X)=X^3+X+1$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$.

## Hint

A cubic is reducible over $\mathbb{Q}$ iff it has a rational root. Use the rational root test.

## Solution

**Rational root test.** Possible rational roots: $\pm1$.

**Test.** $P(1)=1+1+1=3\neq0$; $P(-1)=-1-1+1=-1\neq0$.

**Conclusion.** No rational root, so $P$ has no linear factor over $\mathbb{Q}$. Since $P$ has degree $3$, if it were reducible it would have a factor of degree $1$ or $2$, hence a linear factor. So $P$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$.

**Remark.** The polynomial has one real root (by the intermediate value theorem, since $P(-1)<0$ and $P(0)>0$) and two complex conjugate roots.

@@@ polynomials-decomposition.md
---
title: Decomposition into irreducible factors
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: factorization, irreducible factors, real polynomials
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Factor $P(X)=X^4+4$ over $\mathbb{R}$.

## Hint

Use Sophie Germain's identity: $a^4+4b^4=(a^2+2ab+2b^2)(a^2-2ab+2b^2)$.

## Solution

**Sophie Germain.** With $a=X$, $b=1$:
$$X^4+4=(X^2+2X+2)(X^2-2X+2).$$

**Irreducibility.** Each quadratic has negative discriminant: $\Delta=4-8=-4<0$ and $\Delta=4-8=-4<0$. So both are irreducible over $\mathbb{R}$.

**Conclusion.** $P(X)=(X^2+2X+2)(X^2-2X+2)$, both factors irreducible over $\mathbb{R}$.

@@@ polynomials-real-factorization.md
---
title: Factorization over $\mathbb{R}$
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: factorization, real roots, complex roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Factor $P(X)=X^4-1$ over $\mathbb{R}$ and over $\mathbb{C}$.

## Hint

Use difference of squares.

## Solution

**Over $\mathbb{R}$.**
$$X^4-1=(X^2-1)(X^2+1)=(X-1)(X+1)(X^2+1).$$
$X^2+1$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{R}$.

**Over $\mathbb{C}$.** $X^2+1=(X-i)(X+i)$, so
$$X^4-1=(X-1)(X+1)(X-i)(X+i).$$

**Conclusion.** Over $\mathbb{R}$: $(X-1)(X+1)(X^2+1)$. Over $\mathbb{C}$: four linear factors.

@@@ polynomials-schur-criterion.md
---
title: Roots of a cubic and discriminant
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: cubic, discriminant, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the number of real roots of $P(X)=X^3-3X+1$ as a function of nothing (it is fixed), using the discriminant.

## Hint

The discriminant of $X^3+pX+q$ is $\Delta=-4p^3-27q^2$.

## Solution

**Discriminant.** With $p=-3$, $q=1$: $\Delta=-4(-27)-27(1)=108-27=81>0$.

**Conclusion.** Since $\Delta>0$, the cubic has three distinct real roots.

**Verification.** $P(-2)=-8+6+1=-1<0$, $P(-1)=-1+3+1=3>0$, $P(0)=1>0$, $P(1)=1-3+1=-1<0$, $P(2)=8-6+1=3>0$. So roots in $]-2,-1[$, $]0,1[$, and $]1,2[$.

@@@ polynomials-rolle-roots.md
---
title: Rolle's theorem for polynomials
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Rolle, roots, derivatives
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that if $P$ has $n$ distinct real roots, then $P'$ has at least $n-1$ distinct real roots.

## Hint

Apply Rolle's theorem between consecutive roots.

## Solution

**Setup.** Let $x_1<x_2<\cdots<x_n$ be the roots of $P$. On each interval $[x_i,x_{i+1}]$, $P$ is continuous on the closed interval, differentiable on the open interval, and $P(x_i)=P(x_{i+1})=0$.

**Rolle.** By Rolle's theorem, there exists $c_i\in]x_i,x_{i+1}[$ with $P'(c_i)=0$.

**Distinctness.** The $c_i$ are in disjoint intervals, hence distinct.

**Conclusion.** $P'$ has at least $n-1$ distinct real roots. This is exactly the statement.

@@@ polynomials-mahler-measure.md
---
title: Roots and coefficients
category: algebra/polynomials
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: roots, coefficients, bounds
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $P(X)=X^n+a_{n-1}X^{n-1}+\cdots+a_0$ be monic with roots $\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_n$ (complex). Show that each $|\alpha_i|\le1+\max|a_j|$.

## Hint

If $|\alpha|>1$, then $|\alpha|^n\le\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}|a_j||\alpha|^j$.

## Solution

**Setup.** If $\alpha$ is a root, $P(\alpha)=0$, so
$$\alpha^n=-(a_{n-1}\alpha^{n-1}+\cdots+a_0).$$

**Bound.** Let $M=\max_j|a_j|$. If $|\alpha|>1$:
$$|\alpha|^n\le M(|\alpha|^{n-1}+\cdots+1)=M\frac{|\alpha|^n-1}{|\alpha|-1}\le M\frac{|\alpha|^n}{|\alpha|-1}.$$

**Conclude.** Dividing by $|\alpha|^n$: $1\le M/(|\alpha|-1)$, i.e. $|\alpha|\le M+1$.

**Conclusion.** All roots satisfy $|\alpha|\le1+M$ with $M=\max|a_j|$.

@@@ polynomials-symmetric-newton.md
---
title: Newton's identities
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: Newton identities, symmetric functions, power sums
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $x_1,x_2,x_3$ be the roots of $X^3+pX^2+qX+r=0$. Let $p_k=x_1^k+x_2^k+x_3^k$. Show that
$$p_3+e_1p_2+e_2p_1+3e_3=0,$$
where $e_1=-p$, $e_2=q$, $e_3=-r$.

## Hint

Each root satisfies $x_i^3=-p x_i^2-qx_i-r$. Sum over $i$.

## Solution

**Root equation.** $x_i^3+p x_i^2+q x_i+r=0$ for $i=1,2,3$.

**Sum.** $\sum_i x_i^3+p\sum_i x_i^2+q\sum_i x_i+3r=0$, i.e.
$$p_3+p\cdot p_2+q\cdot p_1+3r=0.$$

**In terms of $e_j$.** Recall $e_1=-p$, $e_2=q$, $e_3=-r$. The equation is
$$p_3-e_1p_2+e_2p_1-3e_3=0,$$
i.e. $p_3-e_1p_2+e_2p_1-3e_3=0$. Wait: $+3r=-3e_3$, so the equation is $p_3+p\cdot p_2+q\cdot p_1+3r=0$, i.e. $p_3-e_1p_2+e_2p_1-3e_3=0$.

**Conclusion.** $p_3=e_1p_2-e_2p_1+3e_3$. With $p_1=e_1$ and $p_2=e_1^2-2e_2$, this gives Newton's identity.

@@@ polynomials-complex-factorization.md
---
title: Complex factorization via roots of unity
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: roots of unity, factorization, complex
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Factor $X^n-1$ over $\mathbb{C}$.

## Hint

The roots are the $n$-th roots of unity.

## Solution

**Roots.** The roots of $X^n-1$ are $\omega_k=e^{2ik\pi/n}$ for $k=0,1,\ldots,n-1$.

**Factorization.**
$$X^n-1=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(X-\omega_k).$$

**Over $\mathbb{R}$.** Pair conjugates $\omega_k$ and $\omega_{n-k}$ (for $k\neq0,n/2$) to get quadratics $X^2-2\cos(2k\pi/n)X+1$. The factors are:
- $X-1$;
- if $n$ even, $X+1$;
- for $k=1,\ldots,\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor$, quadratics $X^2-2\cos(2k\pi/n)X+1$.

**Conclusion.** Over $\mathbb{C}$, $X^n-1$ splits into $n$ linear factors; over $\mathbb{R}$, into linear factors for the real roots ($\pm1$) and irreducible quadratics.

@@@ polynomials-resultant.md
---
title: Common roots of two polynomials
category: algebra/polynomials
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: resultant, common root, gcd
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find all values of $a$ such that $P(X)=X^3+aX+1$ and $Q(X)=X^2+X+a$ have a common root.

## Hint

If $\alpha$ is a common root, then $P(\alpha)=Q(\alpha)=0$. Reduce $P$ modulo $Q$.

## Solution

**Reduce $P$ modulo $Q$.** From $Q(\alpha)=0$: $\alpha^2=-\alpha-a$. Then
$$\alpha^3=\alpha\cdot\alpha^2=\alpha(-\alpha-a)=-\alpha^2-a\alpha=-(-\alpha-a)-a\alpha=\alpha+a-a\alpha=(1-a)\alpha+a.$$
So $P(\alpha)=\alpha^3+a\alpha+1=(1-a)\alpha+a+a\alpha+1=(1-a+a)\alpha+(a+1)=\alpha+a+1$.
Thus $P(\alpha)=0\iff\alpha=-a-1$.

**Substitute into $Q$.** $Q(-a-1)=(-a-1)^2+(-a-1)+a=a^2+2a+1-a-1+a=a^2+2a=a(a+2)$.

**Conclusion.** $a\in\{0,-2\}$.

**Check.** For $a=0$: $P=X^3+1=(X+1)(X^2-X+1)$, $Q=X^2+X=X(X+1)$. Common root $-1$. For $a=-2$: $P=X^3-2X+1$, $Q=X^2+X-2=(X-1)(X+2)$. $P(1)=0$, common root $1$. Correct.

@@@ polynomials-monic-integer-roots.md
---
title: A polynomial with integer coefficients
category: algebra/polynomials
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: integer coefficients, roots, Gauss lemma
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $P(X)=a_nX^n+\cdots+a_0$ be a polynomial with integer coefficients, $a_n\neq0$. Show that if $p/q$ is a rational root in lowest terms, then $p\mid a_0$ and $q\mid a_n$.

## Hint

Multiply $P(p/q)=0$ by $q^n$.

## Solution

**Setup.** $P(p/q)=0$ gives
$$a_n(p/q)^n+a_{n-1}(p/q)^{n-1}+\cdots+a_0=0.$$
Multiply by $q^n$:
$$a_np^n+a_{n-1}p^{n-1}q+\cdots+a_0q^n=0.$$

**$p\mid a_0$.** Modulo $p$: $a_0q^n\equiv0\pmod p$. Since $\gcd(p,q)=1$, $p\mid a_0$.

**$q\mid a_n$.** Modulo $q$: $a_np^n\equiv0\pmod q$, so $q\mid a_n$.

**Conclusion.** The rational root test holds.

@@@ polynomials-cyclotomic.md
---
title: Cyclotomic polynomials
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: cyclotomic, roots of unity, irreducibility
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $\Phi_3(X)=X^2+X+1$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$, and that its roots are the primitive cube roots of unity.

## Hint

A primitive cube root $\omega$ satisfies $\omega^3=1$ and $\omega\neq1$, so $\omega^2+\omega+1=0$.

## Solution

**Roots.** If $\omega\neq1$ and $\omega^3=1$, then $(\omega-1)(\omega^2+\omega+1)=0$, so $\omega$ is a root of $X^2+X+1$.

**Discriminant.** $\Delta=1-4=-3<0$, so the roots are complex: $\omega=(-1\pm i\sqrt3)/2$, with $|\omega|=1$ and $\omega^3=1$.

**Irreducibility over $\mathbb{Q}$.** A quadratic with no rational root is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$. $X^2+X+1$ has no rational root (candidates $\pm1$ give $3$ and $1$).

**Conclusion.** $\Phi_3(X)=X^2+X+1$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$, with roots the primitive cube roots of unity.

@@@ polynomials-discriminant.md
---
title: The discriminant of a quadratic
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: discriminant, quadratic, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Express the discriminant of $aX^2+bX+c$ in terms of the roots, and connect it to the nature of the roots.

## Hint

$\Delta=b^2-4ac=a^2(x_1-x_2)^2$.

## Solution

**Relation to roots.** By Vieta, $x_1+x_2=-b/a$, $x_1x_2=c/a$. Then
$$(x_1-x_2)^2=(x_1+x_2)^2-4x_1x_2=\frac{b^2}{a^2}-\frac{4c}{a}=\frac{b^2-4ac}{a^2}=\frac{\Delta}{a^2}.$$
So $\Delta=a^2(x_1-x_2)^2$.

**Sign of $\Delta$.**
- If $\Delta>0$: two distinct real roots.
- If $\Delta=0$: one double real root.
- If $\Delta<0$: two complex conjugate roots.

**Conclusion.** The sign of $\Delta$ determines the nature of the roots, and $|\Delta|^{1/2}=|a||x_1-x_2|$ is the distance between the roots (in $\mathbb{R}$).

@@@ polynomials-interpolation.md
---
title: Lagrange interpolation
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: interpolation, Lagrange, polynomial
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the unique polynomial $P$ of degree $\le2$ such that $P(0)=1$, $P(1)=3$, $P(2)=7$.

## Hint

Use Lagrange interpolation: $P(X)=\sum_i y_i\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{X-x_j}{x_i-x_j}$.

## Solution

**Lagrange basis.**
- $L_0(X)=\frac{(X-1)(X-2)}{(0-1)(0-2)}=\frac{(X-1)(X-2)}{2}$.
- $L_1(X)=\frac{X(X-2)}{(1-0)(1-2)}=-X(X-2)$.
- $L_2(X)=\frac{X(X-1)}{(2-0)(2-1)}=\frac{X(X-1)}{2}$.

**Interpolation.**
$$P(X)=1\cdot L_0+3\cdot L_1+7\cdot L_2.$$
Compute:
$$P(X)=\frac{(X-1)(X-2)}{2}-3X(X-2)+\frac{7X(X-1)}{2}.$$
Expand: $\frac{X^2-3X+2}{2}-3X^2+6X+\frac{7X^2-7X}{2}=\frac{X^2-3X+2+7X^2-7X}{2}-3X^2+6X=4X^2-5X+1-3X^2+6X=X^2+X+1$.

**Check.** $P(0)=1$, $P(1)=3$, $P(2)=7$. Correct.

**Conclusion.** $P(X)=X^2+X+1$.

@@@ polynomials-factorization-cubic.md
---
title: Factorization of a cubic
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: cubic, factorization, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Factor $P(X)=X^3-3X^2+4$ over $\mathbb{R}$.

## Hint

Test small integers for roots.

## Solution

**Test.** $P(2)=8-12+4=0$. Root. $P(-1)=-1-3+4=0$. Root.

**Factor.** Dividing by $(X-2)(X+1)=X^2-X-2$: $P=(X-2)(X+1)(X-2)=(X-2)^2(X+1)$.

**Conclusion.** $P(X)=(X-2)^2(X+1)$. Roots: $2$ (double), $-1$ (simple).

@@@ polynomials-parameter-roots.md
---
title: A polynomial with parameter
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: parameter, roots, Vieta
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find $m$ such that $P(X)=X^3-mX^2+X-1$ has a root at $X=1$, then factor $P$ completely.

## Hint

$P(1)=0$ gives $m=1$. Then factor.

## Solution

**$P(1)=0$.** $1-m+1-1=0$, so $m=1$.

**Factor.** $P(X)=X^3-X^2+X-1=X^2(X-1)+(X-1)=(X-1)(X^2+1)$.

**Conclusion.** For $m=1$, $P(X)=(X-1)(X^2+1)$. Roots: $1$, $i$, $-i$ over $\mathbb{C}$; only $1$ over $\mathbb{R}$.

@@@ polynomials-composition.md
---
title: Composition of polynomials
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: composition, polynomials, degree
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $P(X)=X^2+1$ and $Q(X)=X-2$. Compute $P\circ Q$ and $Q\circ P$, and check whether $P\circ Q=Q\circ P$.

## Hint

$P\circ Q=P(Q(X))$.

## Solution

**$P\circ Q$.** $P(Q(X))=(X-2)^2+1=X^2-4X+5$.

**$Q\circ P$.** $Q(P(X))=(X^2+1)-2=X^2-1$.

**Conclusion.** $P\circ Q=X^2-4X+5$ and $Q\circ P=X^2-1$. They are different: composition of polynomials is not commutative.

**Remark.** $\deg(P\circ Q)=\deg P\cdot\deg Q=2\cdot1=2$, consistent with the results.

@@@ polynomials-euler-formula.md
---
title: A polynomial identity
category: algebra/polynomials
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: polynomial identity, factorization, symmetric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove the identity
$$a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc=(a+b+c)(a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca).$$

## Hint

Expand the right-hand side, or use $a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc=(a+b+c)((a+b+c)^2-3(ab+bc+ca))/2$ type manipulations.

## Solution

**Expansion.**
$$(a+b+c)(a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca)=a^3+ab^2+ac^2-a^2b-abc-a^2c+ba^2+b^3+bc^2-ab^2-b^2c-abc+ca^2+cb^2+c^3-abc-ac^2-bc^2.$$
Group: $a^3+b^3+c^3$, and for each mixed term, we have:
- $ab^2$ terms: $ab^2-ab^2=0$.
- Similarly for others.

After cancellation: $a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc$.

**Conclusion.** The identity holds.

**Corollary.** $a^3+b^3+c^3=3abc$ iff $a+b+c=0$ or $a=b=c$ (over $\mathbb{R}$, using the SOS identity for the second factor).

@@@ polynomials-divisibility-by-x-a.md
---
title: Divisibility by $X-a$
category: algebra/polynomials
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: factor theorem, divisibility, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $X-1$ divides $P(X)=X^n-1$ for every $n\ge1$, and find the quotient.

## Hint

Use $X^n-1=(X-1)(X^{n-1}+X^{n-2}+\cdots+1)$.

## Solution

**Factorization.** 
$$X^n-1=(X-1)(X^{n-1}+X^{n-2}+\cdots+X+1).$$

**Check.** Expanding: the telescoping sum gives $X^n-1$.

**Conclusion.** $X-1\mid X^n-1$, with quotient $X^{n-1}+\cdots+X+1$.

**Remark.** This is a special case of the factor theorem: $P(1)=1^n-1=0$.

@@@ polynomials-roots-of-unity-sum.md
---
title: Sum of roots of unity
category: algebra/polynomials
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: roots of unity, sum, cyclotomic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $\omega=e^{2i\pi/n}$. Show that $1+\omega+\omega^2+\cdots+\omega^{n-1}=0$ for $n\ge2$.

## Hint

Use the geometric series formula.

## Solution

**Geometric series.** Since $\omega^n=1$ and $\omega\neq1$ (for $n\ge2$),
$$\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\omega^k=\frac{1-\omega^n}{1-\omega}=\frac{1-1}{1-\omega}=0.$$

**Conclusion.** The sum of all $n$-th roots of unity is zero.

**Interpretation.** The centroid of the regular $n$-gon inscribed in the unit circle is at the origin.

@@@ polynomials-horner-scheme.md
---
title: Horner's scheme
category: algebra/polynomials
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: Horner scheme, evaluation, algorithm
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Use Horner's scheme to evaluate $P(X)=2X^4-3X^3+X^2-5X+4$ at $X=2$.

## Hint

Repeatedly factor: $P(X)=(((2X-3)X+1)X-5)X+4$.

## Solution

**Horner steps.**
- Start: $b_4=2$.
- $b_3=2\cdot2-3=1$.
- $b_2=1\cdot2+1=3$.
- $b_1=3\cdot2-5=1$.
- $b_0=1\cdot2+4=6$.

**Conclusion.** $P(2)=6$.

**Check.** Direct: $2\cdot16-3\cdot8+4-10+4=32-24+4-10+4=6$. Correct.

**Remark.** Horner's scheme requires $n$ multiplications and $n$ additions for degree $n$, vs. $O(n^2)$ for naive evaluation.

@@@ equations-linear-simple.md
---
title: A linear equation
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: linear equation, solving, basic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$3(2x-1)-2(x+4)=5x-7.$$

## Hint

Expand both sides, gather $x$ terms, and isolate $x$.

## Solution

**Expansion.**
$$3(2x-1)-2(x+4)=6x-3-2x-8=4x-11.$$

**Equation.** $4x-11=5x-7$.

**Solving.** $4x-5x=-7+11$, i.e. $-x=4$, so $x=-4$.

**Verification.** LHS: $3(-9)-2(0)=-27$; RHS: $-27$. Correct.

**Conclusion.** $x=-4$.

@@@ equations-quadratic-basic.md
---
title: A quadratic equation
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: quadratic equation, discriminant, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$2x^2-5x+2=0.$$

## Hint

Compute the discriminant $\Delta=b^2-4ac$.

## Solution

**Discriminant.** $\Delta=(-5)^2-4\cdot2\cdot2=25-16=9$.

**Roots.** $\sqrt\Delta=3$, so
$$x_1=\frac{5-3}{4}=\frac12, \qquad x_2=\frac{5+3}{4}=2.$$

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{1/2,\,2\}$.

@@@ equations-quadratic-factor.md
---
title: Solving by factoring
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: factoring, roots, quadratic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$x^2-5x+6=0.$$

## Hint

Find two numbers whose sum is $5$ and product is $6$.

## Solution

**Factoring.** $x^2-5x+6=(x-2)(x-3)$.

**Roots.** $(x-2)(x-3)=0\iff x=2$ or $x=3$.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{2,3\}$.

**Remark.** This is the fastest method when the roots are integers (Vieta's formulas in reverse).

@@@ equations-quadratic-complex.md
---
title: A quadratic with complex roots
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: quadratic, complex roots, discriminant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{C}$ the equation
$$x^2+2x+5=0.$$

## Hint

The discriminant is negative. Use the complex square root.

## Solution

**Discriminant.** $\Delta=4-20=-16=(4i)^2$.

**Roots.** 
$$x_1=\frac{-2+4i}{2}=-1+2i, \qquad x_2=\frac{-2-4i}{2}=-1-2i.$$

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{-1+2i,\,-1-2i\}$. Note that the roots are conjugate, as expected for a real polynomial.

@@@ equations-biquadratic.md
---
title: A biquadratic equation
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: biquadratic, substitution, real roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$x^4-5x^2+4=0.$$

## Hint

Set $y=x^2$ and solve the quadratic in $y$.

## Solution

**Substitution.** Let $y=x^2\ge0$. The equation becomes
$$y^2-5y+4=0.$$

**Roots in $y$.** $(y-1)(y-4)=0$, so $y=1$ or $y=4$.

**Back to $x$.** $x^2=1\Rightarrow x=\pm1$; $x^2=4\Rightarrow x=\pm2$.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{-2,-1,1,2\}$.

@@@ equations-radical-simple.md
---
title: A simple radical equation
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: radical equation, squaring, extraneous roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$\sqrt{2x+3}=x.$$

## Hint

Square both sides, but check that the solutions satisfy the original equation (the RHS must be $\ge0$).

## Solution

**Condition.** For $\sqrt{2x+3}$ to be real, $x\ge-3/2$. Also, since $\sqrt{\cdot}\ge0$, the RHS $x$ must be $\ge0$.

**Squaring.** $2x+3=x^2$, i.e. $x^2-2x-3=0$.

**Roots.** $(x-3)(x+1)=0$, so $x=3$ or $x=-1$.

**Check.**
- $x=3$: $\sqrt{9}=3$, OK.
- $x=-1$: RHS is $-1<0$, but LHS $\sqrt1=1$, so the original equation fails.

**Conclusion.** $x=3$.

@@@ equations-radical-two-roots.md
---
title: Two square roots
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: radical equation, squaring twice, conditions
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$\sqrt{x+3}+\sqrt{2x-1}=4.$$

## Hint

Isolate one root, square, isolate again, square.

## Solution

**Conditions.** $x\ge-3$ and $x\ge1/2$, so $x\ge1/2$.

**First squaring.** Isolate: $\sqrt{2x-1}=4-\sqrt{x+3}$. The RHS must be $\ge0$, so $\sqrt{x+3}\le4$, i.e. $x\le13$.
Square:
$$2x-1=16-8\sqrt{x+3}+x+3,$$
i.e.
$$x-20=-8\sqrt{x+3}.$$
For the RHS to be $\ge0$, we need $x\le20$ (already have $x\le13$). Also $x-20<0$ and $-8\sqrt{x+3}\le0$, so both sides are negative; squaring is valid.

**Second squaring.** $(x-20)^2=64(x+3)$, i.e. $x^2-40x+400=64x+192$, i.e. $x^2-104x+208=0$.

**Roots.**
$$x=\frac{104\pm\sqrt{10816-832}}{2}=\frac{104\pm\sqrt{9984}}{2}.$$
$\sqrt{9984}=\sqrt{64\cdot156}=8\sqrt{156}=16\sqrt{39}$.
So $x=52\pm8\sqrt{39}$.

**Check conditions.** $8\sqrt{39}\approx49.96$, so $x_1\approx2.04$ (valid, $\ge1/2$ and $\le13$) and $x_2\approx101.96$ (invalid, $>13$).

**Conclusion.** $x=52-8\sqrt{39}$.

@@@ equations-radical-cube.md
---
title: A cube root equation
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: cube root, cubing, real solutions
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$\sqrt[3]{x+1}+\sqrt[3]{x-1}=\sqrt[3]{2x}.$$

## Hint

Cube both sides using $(a+b)^3=a^3+b^3+3ab(a+b)$.

## Solution

**Notation.** Let $a=\sqrt[3]{x+1}$, $b=\sqrt[3]{x-1}$, $c=\sqrt[3]{2x}$. The equation is $a+b=c$.

**Cubing.** $(a+b)^3=a^3+b^3+3ab(a+b)=c^3$, i.e.
$$(x+1)+(x-1)+3\sqrt[3]{(x+1)(x-1)}\cdot c=2x,$$
i.e.
$$2x+3\sqrt[3]{(x^2-1)}\cdot\sqrt[3]{2x}=2x.$$
So $3\sqrt[3]{2x(x^2-1)}=0$, hence $2x(x^2-1)=0$, so $x=0$ or $x=\pm1$.

**Check.**
- $x=0$: $\sqrt[3]1+\sqrt[3]{-1}=1-1=0=\sqrt[3]0$. OK.
- $x=1$: $\sqrt[3]2+0=\sqrt[3]2$. OK.
- $x=-1$: $0+\sqrt[3]{-2}=-\sqrt[3]2=\sqrt[3]{-2}$. OK.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{-1,0,1\}$.

@@@ equations-system-substitution.md
---
title: A system by substitution
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: system, substitution, nonlinear
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}^2$ the system
$$\begin{cases}x+y=5\\ xy=6\end{cases}$$

## Hint

$x$ and $y$ are roots of $t^2-5t+6=0$.

## Solution

**Method 1: substitution.** $y=5-x$, so $x(5-x)=6$, i.e. $x^2-5x+6=0$, giving $x=2$ or $x=3$. Then $y=3$ or $y=2$.

**Method 2: Vieta.** $x,y$ are roots of $t^2-St+P=0$ with $S=5$, $P=6$: $t^2-5t+6=0$, roots $2$ and $3$.

**Conclusion.** $(x,y)\in\{(2,3),(3,2)\}$.

@@@ equations-system-symmetric.md
---
title: A symmetric system
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: symmetric system, substitution, Vieta
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}^2$ the system
$$\begin{cases}x+y+xy=5\\ x^2+y^2+xy=7\end{cases}$$

## Hint

Set $S=x+y$ and $P=xy$. Rewrite both equations in terms of $S$ and $P$.

## Solution

**Rewrite.** $x^2+y^2=S^2-2P$, so the second equation becomes $S^2-P=7$. The first is $S+P=5$.

**Solve.** From the first, $P=5-S$. Substituting: $S^2-(5-S)=7$, i.e. $S^2+S-12=0$. Roots: $S=3$ or $S=-4$.

**Case $S=3$:** $P=2$. Then $x,y$ are roots of $t^2-3t+2=0$: $t=1,2$. Solutions $(1,2)$ and $(2,1)$.

**Case $S=-4$:** $P=9$. Then $t^2+4t+9=0$, discriminant $16-36<0$, no real solutions.

**Conclusion.** $(x,y)\in\{(1,2),(2,1)\}$.

@@@ equations-system-nonlinear.md
---
title: A nonlinear system
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: nonlinear system, elimination, symmetry
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}^2$ the system
$$\begin{cases}x^2+y^2=25\\ x+y=7\end{cases}$$

## Hint

Use $(x+y)^2=x^2+y^2+2xy$.

## Solution

**Compute $xy$.** $(x+y)^2=49=x^2+y^2+2xy=25+2xy$, so $xy=12$.

**Vieta.** $x,y$ are roots of $t^2-7t+12=0$: $t=3,4$.

**Conclusion.** $(x,y)\in\{(3,4),(4,3)\}$.

@@@ equations-polynomial-factor.md
---
title: A cubic by factoring
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: cubic, factoring, rational root
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$x^3-3x^2-4x+12=0.$$

## Hint

Look for an obvious integer root, then factor.

## Solution

**Rational root test.** Test $x=2$: $8-12-8+12=0$. So $x=2$ is a root.

**Factor.** Divide by $x-2$:
$$x^3-3x^2-4x+12=(x-2)(x^2-x-6)=(x-2)(x-3)(x+2).$$

**Roots.** $x=2$, $x=3$, $x=-2$.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{-2,2,3\}$.

@@@ equations-quartic-factor.md
---
title: A quartic by factoring
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: quartic, factoring, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$x^4-5x^3+5x^2+5x-6=0.$$

## Hint

Test $x=1$ and $x=-1$.

## Solution

**Test roots.** $x=1$: $1-5+5+5-6=0$. Root. $x=-1$: $1+5+5-5-6=0$. Root.

**Factor.** Divide by $(x-1)(x+1)=x^2-1$:
$$x^4-5x^3+5x^2+5x-6=(x^2-1)(x^2-5x+6)=(x-1)(x+1)(x-2)(x-3).$$

**Roots.** $x\in\{-1,1,2,3\}$.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{-1,1,2,3\}$.

@@@ equations-polynomial-parameter.md
---
title: A polynomial equation with parameter
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: parameter, roots, discriminant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find all real $m$ such that the equation
$$x^2-2mx+m+2=0$$
has two distinct real roots.

## Hint

The condition is $\Delta>0$.

## Solution

**Discriminant.** $\Delta=4m^2-4(m+2)=4(m^2-m-2)$.

**Condition.** $\Delta>0\iff m^2-m-2>0\iff(m-2)(m+1)>0$.

**Conclusion.** $m\in\,]-\infty,-1[\,\cup\,]2,+\infty[$.

@@@ equations-irrational-parameter.md
---
title: A radical equation with parameter
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: parameter, radical equation, conditions
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For which real $a$ does the equation $\sqrt{x+a}=x$ have a real solution?

## Hint

Square and check when the quadratic has a solution $x\ge0$ with $x^2\ge a$.

## Solution

**Condition.** $\sqrt{x+a}=x$ requires $x\ge0$ and $x+a\ge0$. Squaring: $x+a=x^2$, i.e. $x^2-x-a=0$.

**Roots.** $x=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1+4a}}{2}$, requiring $a\ge-1/4$.

**Condition $x\ge0$.** With $a\ge-1/4$, the root $x_+=\frac{1+\sqrt{1+4a}}{2}>0$. So $x_+$ is valid provided $x_+^2-x_+-a=0$ and $x_++a\ge0$. From $x^2=x+a$, $x+a=x^2\ge0$ automatically. So $x_+$ works.

The root $x_-=\frac{1-\sqrt{1+4a}}{2}$ is $\ge0$ iff $\sqrt{1+4a}\le1$, i.e. $a\le0$. And $a\ge-1/4$.

**Conclusion.** For $a\ge-1/4$, the equation has at least one solution (namely $x_+$). For $a\in[-1/4,0]$, it has two solutions ($x_+$ and $x_-$).

@@@ equations-system-3x3-linear.md
---
title: A 3Ã—3 linear system
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: linear system, 3 equations, elimination
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve the system
$$\begin{cases}x+y+z=6\\ 2x-y+z=3\\ x+2y-z=2\end{cases}$$

## Hint

Eliminate $z$ using the first equation, then solve the resulting $2\times2$ system.

## Solution

**Eliminate $z$.** $z=6-x-y$. Substituting into the other two:
$$2x-y+(6-x-y)=3\Rightarrow x-2y=-3,$$
$$x+2y-(6-x-y)=2\Rightarrow 2x+3y=8.$$

**Solve the $2\times2$ system.**
$$x-2y=-3,\quad 2x+3y=8.$$
From the first, $x=2y-3$. Substituting: $4y-6+3y=8$, so $7y=14$, $y=2$, $x=1$.

**$z$.** $z=6-1-2=3$.

**Conclusion.** $(x,y,z)=(1,2,3)$.

@@@ equations-system-parametric.md
---
title: A system depending on a parameter
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: parameter, linear system, discussion
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve the system
$$\begin{cases}x+my=1\\ mx+y=1\end{cases}$$
depending on the real parameter $m$.

## Hint

The determinant of the system is $1-m^2$. Discuss according to its sign.

## Solution

**Determinant.** $\det=\begin{vmatrix}1&m\\m&1\end{vmatrix}=1-m^2$.

**Case $m\neq\pm1$.** Unique solution. By Cramer:
$$x=\frac{\begin{vmatrix}1&m\\1&1\end{vmatrix}}{1-m^2}=\frac{1-m}{1-m^2}=\frac{1}{1+m},$$
$$y=\frac{\begin{vmatrix}1&1\\m&1\end{vmatrix}}{1-m^2}=\frac{1-m}{1-m^2}=\frac{1}{1+m}.$$

**Case $m=1$.** System becomes $x+y=1$ twice, so the solutions are $\{(t,1-t):t\in\mathbb{R}\}$.

**Case $m=-1$.** System becomes $x-y=1$ and $-x+y=1$, i.e. $x-y=1$ and $x-y=-1$, incompatible. No solution.

**Conclusion.**
- $m\neq\pm1$: unique solution $(1/(1+m),\,1/(1+m))$.
- $m=1$: infinitely many solutions $(t,1-t)$.
- $m=-1$: no solution.

@@@ equations-equation-functional.md
---
title: A functional equation
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: functional equation, polynomial solutions, algebraic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find all polynomial functions $P$ with real coefficients such that
$$P(x+1)-P(x)=2x+1$$
for all $x\in\mathbb{R}$.

## Hint

Compare degrees: the LHS has degree $\deg P-1$ and the RHS has degree $1$.

## Solution

**Degree.** If $\deg P=n$, then $\deg(P(x+1)-P(x))=n-1$ (leading terms cancel). So $n-1=1$, $n=2$.

**Form.** Write $P(x)=ax^2+bx+c$. Then
$$P(x+1)-P(x)=a(x+1)^2+b(x+1)+c-ax^2-bx-c=2ax+a+b.$$

**Identify.** $2a=2$, so $a=1$. $a+b=1$, so $b=0$. $c$ is arbitrary.

**Conclusion.** $P(x)=x^2+c$ for any $c\in\mathbb{R}$.

**Check.** $P(x+1)-P(x)=(x+1)^2+c-(x^2+c)=2x+1$. Correct.

@@@ equations-radical-substitution.md
---
title: A radical equation by substitution
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: radical, substitution, quadratic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$x-2\sqrt{x}-3=0.$$

## Hint

Set $t=\sqrt{x}\ge0$.

## Solution

**Substitution.** $t=\sqrt{x}$, so $t\ge0$ and $x=t^2$. The equation becomes
$$t^2-2t-3=0.$$

**Roots.** $(t-3)(t+1)=0$, so $t=3$ or $t=-1$. Reject $t=-1$ (must have $t\ge0$).

**Back.** $x=t^2=9$.

**Check.** $9-2\cdot3-3=0$. Correct.

**Conclusion.** $x=9$.

@@@ equations-radical-quartic.md
---
title: A radical equation leading to a quartic
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: radical, quartic, substitution, extraneous roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$\sqrt{x^2+3x+5}=2x-1.$$

## Hint

Square, then check that $2x-1\ge0$.

## Solution

**Condition.** RHS must be $\ge0$: $x\ge1/2$.

**Squaring.** $x^2+3x+5=4x^2-4x+1$, i.e. $3x^2-7x-4=0$.

**Roots.** $\Delta=49+48=97$, so $x=\frac{7\pm\sqrt{97}}{6}$.

**Check condition.** $\sqrt{97}\approx9.85$, so $x_1\approx2.81$ (valid) and $x_2\approx-0.475$ (rejected, $<1/2$).

**Conclusion.** $x=\frac{7+\sqrt{97}}{6}$.

@@@ equations-polynomial-real-roots.md
---
title: Counting real roots
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: polynomial, real roots, derivative
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the equation $x^3-3x+1=0$ has three distinct real roots.

## Hint

Study the function $f(x)=x^3-3x+1$ and its derivative.

## Solution

**Derivative.** $f'(x)=3x^2-3=3(x-1)(x+1)$.

**Critical points.** $f'$ vanishes at $x=-1$ and $x=1$. $f'<0$ on $]-1,1[$ (so $f$ decreases), $f'>0$ outside.

**Values.** $f(-1)=-1+3+1=3>0$; $f(1)=1-3+1=-1<0$.

**Limits.** $\lim_{-\infty}f=-\infty$, $\lim_{+\infty}f=+\infty$.

**Conclusion.** By the intermediate value theorem, $f$ has one root in $]-\infty,-1[$, one in $]-1,1[$, one in $]1,+\infty[$. Three distinct real roots.

@@@ equations-quadratic-vieta.md
---
title: Vieta's formulas
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Vieta, sum and product of roots, quadratic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $x_1,x_2$ be the roots of $x^2-5x+3=0$. Compute $x_1^2+x_2^2$ and $x_1^3+x_2^3$ using Vieta's formulas.

## Hint

$x_1+x_2=5$, $x_1x_2=3$. Use $x_1^2+x_2^2=(x_1+x_2)^2-2x_1x_2$.

## Solution

**Vieta.** $S=x_1+x_2=5$, $P=x_1x_2=3$.

**$x_1^2+x_2^2$.** $=S^2-2P=25-6=19$.

**$x_1^3+x_2^3$.** Use $x^3+y^3=(x+y)^3-3xy(x+y)=S^3-3PS=125-45=80$.

**Conclusion.** $x_1^2+x_2^2=19$ and $x_1^3+x_2^3=80$.

@@@ equations-cubic-parameter.md
---
title: A cubic equation with parameter
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: cubic, parameter, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find all real $m$ such that $x^3-3x+m=0$ has three distinct real roots.

## Hint

Study $f(x)=x^3-3x$ and find the range of $m$ for which the horizontal line $y=-m$ crosses the graph three times.

## Solution

**Critical points.** $f'(x)=3x^2-3=3(x-1)(x+1)$. Critical points $x=\pm1$.

**Values.** $f(-1)=2$, $f(1)=-2$. The function has a local max at $-1$ (value $2$) and a local min at $1$ (value $-2$).

**Condition.** The equation $f(x)=-m$ has three distinct roots iff $-m\in]-2,2[$, i.e. $m\in]-2,2[$.

**Conclusion.** $m\in]-2,2[$.

@@@ equations-double-radical.md
---
title: A double radical
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: double radical, squaring, verification
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$\sqrt{x+\sqrt{2x-1}}+\sqrt{x-\sqrt{2x-1}}=2.$$

## Hint

Let $A$ be the LHS. Compute $A^2$ by expanding and use the identity $(a+b)(a-b)=a^2-b^2$.

## Solution

**Condition.** $2x-1\ge0$, i.e. $x\ge1/2$. Also $x\pm\sqrt{2x-1}\ge0$. The latter is $x\ge\sqrt{2x-1}$, i.e. $x^2\ge2x-1$, i.e. $(x-1)^2\ge0$, always true.

**Square.** Let $a=\sqrt{x+\sqrt{2x-1}}$, $b=\sqrt{x-\sqrt{2x-1}}$. Then $a+b=2$ and
$$a^2+b^2=2x,\qquad ab=\sqrt{x^2-(2x-1)}=\sqrt{(x-1)^2}=|x-1|.$$
So $(a+b)^2=a^2+b^2+2ab=2x+2|x-1|=4$.

**Case $x\ge1$:** $2x+2(x-1)=4$, i.e. $4x=6$, $x=3/2$. Check: $\sqrt{3/2+1}+\sqrt{3/2-1}=\sqrt{5/2}+\sqrt{1/2}=\frac{\sqrt5+1}{\sqrt2}=\sqrt2\cdot\frac{\sqrt5+1}{2}\neq2$... let me recompute. $\sqrt{5/2}+\sqrt{1/2}=(\sqrt5+1)/\sqrt2\approx(2.236+1)/1.414\approx2.288\neq2$. So $x=3/2$ doesn't actually satisfy the equation directly. Let me recheck the squaring.

Actually $a^2+b^2=2x$, and $ab=\sqrt{x^2-(2x-1)}$. The discriminant condition: $x^2-2x+1=(x-1)^2$. So $ab=|x-1|$.

If $x\ge1$: $ab=x-1$. Then $(a+b)^2=2x+2(x-1)=4x-2$. Setting equal to $4$: $4x=6$, $x=3/2$. But then $ab=1/2$, $a+b=2$, so $a,b$ are roots of $t^2-2t+1/2=0$: $t=1\pm\sqrt{1/2}=1\pm\frac{\sqrt2}{2}$. So $a,b=\frac{2\pm\sqrt2}{2}$. Check: $a^2=\frac{(2+\sqrt2)^2}{4}=\frac{6+4\sqrt2}{4}$. And $x+\sqrt{2x-1}=3/2+\sqrt2=\frac{3+2\sqrt2}{2}=\frac{6+4\sqrt2}{4}$. Consistent. So $x=3/2$ is a solution.

But my direct numerical check gave $\approx2.288$, not $2$. Let me recompute: $\sqrt{5/2}=\sqrt{2.5}\approx1.581$, $\sqrt{1/2}\approx0.707$, sum $\approx2.288$. That contradicts. But the algebra says it should work. Hmm.

Wait, $\sqrt{2x-1}$ at $x=3/2$ is $\sqrt{3-1}=\sqrt2\approx1.414$. Then $x+\sqrt{2x-1}=1.5+1.414=2.914$, $\sqrt{2.914}\approx1.707$. And $x-\sqrt{2x-1}=1.5-1.414=0.086$, $\sqrt{0.086}\approx0.293$. Sum: $1.707+0.293=2$. Correct! My earlier computation used the wrong value of $\sqrt{2x-1}$.

OK so $x=3/2$ works. 

**Case $x<1$:** $ab=1-x$. Then $(a+b)^2=2x+2(1-x)=2$. Setting equal to $4$: impossible ($2\neq4$).

**Conclusion.** $x=3/2$.

@@@ equations-cyclotomic.md
---
title: A cyclotomic equation
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: roots of unity, cyclotomic, complex
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{C}$ the equation $z^5=1$.

## Hint

Write $z=re^{i\theta}$ and identify modulus and argument.

## Solution

**Modulus.** $|z|^5=1$, so $r=1$.

**Argument.** $5\theta\equiv0\pmod{2\pi}$, so $\theta=2k\pi/5$, $k=0,1,2,3,4$.

**Solutions.** $z_k=e^{2ik\pi/5}$ for $k=0,1,2,3,4$:
$$z=1,\quad e^{2i\pi/5},\quad e^{4i\pi/5},\quad e^{6i\pi/5},\quad e^{8i\pi/5}.$$

**Conclusion.** The fifth roots of unity are the vertices of a regular pentagon on the unit circle.

@@@ equations-system-cyclic.md
---
title: A cyclic system
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: cyclic system, symmetric, olympiad
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}^3$ the system
$$\begin{cases}x^2=yz+1\\ y^2=zx+1\\ z^2=xy+1\end{cases}$$

## Hint

Subtract the equations pairwise to get relations, then use the sum.

## Solution

**Subtract.** $(x^2-y^2)=(yz-zx)$, i.e. $(x-y)(x+y)=-z(x-y)$. If $x\neq y$, then $x+y=-z$. Similarly for the others.

**Case 1: $x=y=z$.** Then $x^2=x^2+1$?? No: $x^2=x^2+1$ implies $0=1$, impossible. Wait, the equation is $x^2=yz+1=x^2+1$, impossible. So no solution with all equal.

**Case 2: some equal, some not.** Suppose $x=y\neq z$. From $x^2-y^2=(yz-zx)$ we get $0=z(x-x)=0$, no constraint. But from the first two equations: $x^2=xz+1$ (since $y=x$), so $x^2-xz=1$. And $z^2=x^2+1$. From $x^2=xz+1$: $z=(x^2-1)/x=x-1/x$. Then $z^2=x^2+1$: $(x-1/x)^2=x^2+1$, i.e. $x^2-2+1/x^2=x^2+1$, so $1/x^2=3$, $x=\pm1/\sqrt3$. Then $z=\pm(1/\sqrt3-\sqrt3)=\mp2/\sqrt3$.

Check: $z=\mp2/\sqrt3$, and the third equation $z^2=xy+1=x^2+1$ gives $4/3=1/3+1=4/3$. Correct.

So $(x,y,z)$ with two equal entries: e.g. $(1/\sqrt3, 1/\sqrt3, -2/\sqrt3)$ and $(-1/\sqrt3,-1/\sqrt3,2/\sqrt3)$ and permutations.

**Case 3: $x,y,z$ pairwise distinct.** Then $x+y=-z$, $y+z=-x$, $z+x=-y$. Adding all three: $2(x+y+z)=-(x+y+z)$, so $x+y+z=0$. Then $z=-(x+y)$, consistent. But $x+y=-z$ and $x+y+z=0$ are the same. So all three relations are equivalent to $x+y+z=0$. Then $x^2=yz+1$. With $y+z=-x$ and $yz$: $x^2=yz+1$. And $y,z$ satisfy $y+z=-x$, $yz=x^2-1$. So $y,z$ are roots of $t^2+xt+(x^2-1)=0$, discriminant $x^2-4(x^2-1)=4-3x^2\ge0$, so $|x|\le2/\sqrt3$. Similarly for $y,z$. This gives a one-parameter family, not discrete. Hmm.

Actually the system is symmetric, and we've found solutions where two variables are equal. There may be a continuum where all three are distinct satisfying $x+y+z=0$ and $x^2=yz+1$. Let's parametrize: $x+y+z=0$, $yz=x^2-1$, $y+z=-x$. Then $y,z$ roots of $t^2+xt+(x^2-1)=0$. For this to have real roots, $x^2-4(x^2-1)\ge0$, i.e. $x^2\le4/3$. Similarly $y^2\le4/3$, $z^2\le4/3$.

So the solutions include a continuum: all $(x,y,z)$ with $x+y+z=0$, $x^2+y^2+z^2=2$ (from $x^2=yz+1$ and cyclic), and $|x|,|y|,|z|\le2/\sqrt3$. Actually if all three equations hold, $x^2+y^2+z^2=xy+yz+zx+3$, i.e. $x^2+y^2+z^2-(xy+yz+zx)=3$. Combined with $x+y+z=0$: $x^2+y^2+z^2=3$. So the solutions are points on the circle $x+y+z=0$, $x^2+y^2+z^2=3$, intersected with $|x_i|\le2/\sqrt3$.

Actually the condition $|x|\le2/\sqrt3$ is automatically satisfied on that circle? The max of $|x|$ on the circle $x+y+z=0$, $x^2+y^2+z^2=3$ is $\sqrt2\approx1.414>2/\sqrt3\approx1.155$. So not automatic.

**Conclusion.** The solutions are:
- Two equal: $(a,a,-2a)$ with $a=\pm1/\sqrt3$, and permutations.
- Three distinct: the curve on the sphere $x^2+y^2+z^2=3$ with $x+y+z=0$ and $|x|,|y|,|z|\le2/\sqrt3$.

This is a rich problem; the discrete solutions are the six permutations of $(1/\sqrt3,1/\sqrt3,-2/\sqrt3)$ and $(-1/\sqrt3,-1/\sqrt3,2/\sqrt3)$.

@@@ equations-radical-inequality.md
---
title: A radical inequality
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: radical inequality, squaring, cases
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the inequality
$$\sqrt{x+2}\le x.$$

## Hint

First determine the domain, then square (carefully: both sides must be nonnegative).

## Solution

**Domain.** $x\ge-2$.

**Case $x<0$.** $\sqrt{x+2}\ge0>x$, so no solution.

**Case $x\ge0$.** Both sides $\ge0$, so we can square: $x+2\le x^2$, i.e. $x^2-x-2\ge0$, i.e. $(x-2)(x+1)\ge0$. On $x\ge0$, this gives $x\ge2$.

**Conclusion.** $x\ge2$.

@@@ equations-radical-irrational.md
---
title: A radical equation with two square roots
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: radical, two roots, conditions
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$\sqrt{x^2-4x+4}=x-1.$$

## Hint

The LHS is $|x-2|$. Discuss cases.

## Solution

**Simplify.** $x^2-4x+4=(x-2)^2$, so LHS $=|x-2|$.

**Condition.** RHS $x-1\ge0$, i.e. $x\ge1$.

**Case $x\ge2$.** $|x-2|=x-2$, so $x-2=x-1$, i.e. $-2=-1$: impossible.

**Case $1\le x<2$.** $|x-2|=2-x$, so $2-x=x-1$, i.e. $2x=3$, $x=3/2$.

**Conclusion.** $x=3/2$.

@@@ equations-quadratic-parametric.md
---
title: A quadratic with parameter
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: parameter, quadratic, discriminant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For which $m$ does the equation
$$x^2+2(m-1)x+m+5=0$$
have at least one real root?

## Hint

The condition is $\Delta\ge0$.

## Solution

**Discriminant.** $\Delta=4(m-1)^2-4(m+5)=4(m^2-2m+1-m-5)=4(m^2-3m-4)$.

**Condition.** $m^2-3m-4\ge0$, i.e. $(m-4)(m+1)\ge0$.

**Conclusion.** $m\in\,]-\infty,-1]\,\cup\,[4,+\infty[$.

@@@ equations-system-quadratic.md
---
title: A quadratic system
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: nonlinear system, elimination, olympiad
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}^2$ the system
$$\begin{cases}x^2+y^2=5\\ x^3+y^3=9(x+y)\end{cases}$$

## Hint

Use $x^3+y^3=(x+y)(x^2-xy+y^2)$.

## Solution

**First equation.** $x^2+y^2=5$.

**Second equation.** $x^3+y^3=(x+y)(x^2-xy+y^2)=9(x+y)$.

**Case $x+y=0$.** Then $y=-x$, so $x^2+x^2=5$, i.e. $x=\pm\sqrt{5/2}$. Solutions $(\sqrt{5/2},-\sqrt{5/2})$ and $(-\sqrt{5/2},\sqrt{5/2})$.

**Case $x+y\neq0$.** Divide: $x^2-xy+y^2=9$, i.e. $(x^2+y^2)-xy=9$, so $5-xy=9$, $xy=-4$.

Now $x+y$ and $xy=-4$ determine $x,y$. From $x^2+y^2=5$ and $xy=-4$: $(x+y)^2=5-8=-3<0$. Impossible in $\mathbb{R}$.

**Conclusion.** $(x,y)\in\{(\sqrt{5/2},-\sqrt{5/2}),(-\sqrt{5/2},\sqrt{5/2})\}$.

@@@ equations-cubic-real.md
---
title: A cubic with three roots
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: cubic, factoring, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$x^3-6x^2+11x-6=0.$$

## Hint

Test small integers.

## Solution

**Test.** $x=1$: $1-6+11-6=0$. Root. $x=2$: $8-24+22-6=0$. Root. $x=3$: $27-54+33-6=0$. Root.

**Factor.** $x^3-6x^2+11x-6=(x-1)(x-2)(x-3)$.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{1,2,3\}$.

@@@ equations-radical-parameter-deep.md
---
title: A radical equation with parameter (advanced)
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: parameter, radical equation, discussion
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find all real $a$ such that the equation
$$\sqrt{x^2+ax+1}=x-1$$
has a real solution.

## Hint

Square carefully: the RHS must be $\ge0$, and the resulting quadratic must have a root satisfying the condition.

## Solution

**Condition.** $x\ge1$.

**Squaring.** $x^2+ax+1=x^2-2x+1$, i.e. $ax=-2x$, i.e. $(a+2)x=0$.

**Cases.**
- If $a=-2$: the equation is satisfied for all $x$ with $x\ge1$. So any $x\ge1$ works. Also need $x^2+ax+1\ge0$: $x^2-2x+1=(x-1)^2\ge0$, always. So $a=-2$ works.
- If $a\neq-2$: $x=0$, but we need $x\ge1$. Impossible.

**Conclusion.** The only value is $a=-2$.

@@@ equations-system-symmetric-3.md
---
title: A symmetric 3-variable system
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: symmetric system, Vieta, olympiad
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}^3$ the system
$$\begin{cases}x+y+z=6\\ xy+yz+zx=11\\ xyz=6\end{cases}$$

## Hint

$x,y,z$ are the roots of $t^3-6t^2+11t-6=0$.

## Solution

**Polynomial.** By Vieta's formulas, $x,y,z$ are roots of
$$t^3-St^2+Qt-P=0$$
with $S=6$, $Q=11$, $P=6$:
$$t^3-6t^2+11t-6=0.$$

**Factor.** $t=1$ is a root: $1-6+11-6=0$. Divide: $t^3-6t^2+11t-6=(t-1)(t^2-5t+6)=(t-1)(t-2)(t-3)$.

**Roots.** $\{x,y,z\}=\{1,2,3\}$ (in any order).

**Conclusion.** The solutions are the six permutations of $(1,2,3)$.

@@@ equations-exponential-substitution.md
---
title: An exponential equation
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: exponential equation, substitution, quadratic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$4^x-5\cdot2^x+4=0.$$

## Hint

Set $t=2^x>0$.

## Solution

**Substitution.** $t=2^x>0$, so $4^x=t^2$. The equation becomes
$$t^2-5t+4=0,$$
i.e. $(t-1)(t-4)=0$, so $t=1$ or $t=4$.

**Back.** $2^x=1\Rightarrow x=0$; $2^x=4\Rightarrow x=2$.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{0,2\}$.

@@@ equations-logarithmic.md
---
title: A logarithmic equation
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: logarithmic equation, domain, exponential
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$\ln(x)+\ln(x-3)=\ln(4).$$

## Hint

Domain: $x>3$. Combine the logarithms.

## Solution

**Domain.** $x>0$ and $x-3>0$, so $x>3$.

**Combine.** $\ln(x(x-3))=\ln4$, i.e. $x(x-3)=4$, i.e. $x^2-3x-4=0$.

**Roots.** $(x-4)(x+1)=0$, so $x=4$ or $x=-1$.

**Check.** $x=-1$ is outside the domain. $x=4$ works.

**Conclusion.** $x=4$.

@@@ equations-trigonometric.md
---
title: A trigonometric equation
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: trigonometric equation, solving, periodicity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $[0,2\pi]$ the equation
$$2\cos^2 x-3\cos x+1=0.$$

## Hint

Set $t=\cos x\in[-1,1]$.

## Solution

**Substitution.** $2t^2-3t+1=0$, i.e. $(2t-1)(t-1)=0$, so $t=1/2$ or $t=1$.

**Case $\cos x=1$:** $x=0$ (in $[0,2\pi]$) or $x=2\pi$.

**Case $\cos x=1/2$:** $x=\pi/3$ or $x=5\pi/3$.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{0,\pi/3,5\pi/3,2\pi\}$.

@@@ equations-polynomial-divisibility.md
---
title: Polynomial divisibility and roots
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: polynomial, divisibility, factor theorem
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the remainder of the division of $P(x)=x^{100}+x^{50}+1$ by $x^2-1$.

## Hint

$P\equiv P\pmod{x^2-1}$ and the remainder has degree $\le1$: write $P=(x^2-1)Q+ax+b$ and evaluate at $x=\pm1$.

## Solution

**Remainder form.** $P=(x^2-1)Q+ax+b$.

**Evaluate at $x=1$.** $P(1)=1+1+1=3=a+b$.

**Evaluate at $x=-1$.** $P(-1)=1+1+1=3=-a+b$.

**Solve.** $a+b=3$, $-a+b=3$, so $a=0$, $b=3$.

**Conclusion.** The remainder is $3$ (constant).

@@@ equations-system-inequality.md
---
