title: A system with an inequality
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: system, inequality, integers
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find all integers $(x,y)$ satisfying
$$\begin{cases}x+y=5\\ x^2+y^2\le13\end{cases}$$

## Hint

List possibilities for $(x,y)$ with $x+y=5$, then check.

## Solution

**From $x+y=5$:** $y=5-x$. Then
$$x^2+(5-x)^2\le13\iff2x^2-10x+25\le13\iff2x^2-10x+12\le0\iff x^2-5x+6\le0.$$
This is $(x-2)(x-3)\le0$, so $x\in[2,3]$. Integer $x\in\{2,3\}$.

**Solutions.** $(2,3)$ and $(3,2)$.

**Conclusion.** $(x,y)\in\{(2,3),(3,2)\}$.

@@@ equations-equation-with-floor.md
---
title: An equation with the floor function
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: floor function, integer part, olympiad
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$\lfloor x\rfloor + x = 5.$$

## Hint

Write $x=n+r$ with $n=\lfloor x\rfloor\in\mathbb{Z}$ and $r\in[0,1[$.

## Solution

**Notation.** $x=n+r$ with $n=\lfloor x\rfloor$, $0\le r<1$. The equation is
$$n+(n+r)=5\iff2n+r=5.$$

**Consequences.** Since $0\le r<1$, we have $5-1<2n\le5$, i.e. $2< n\le2.5$. Since $n\in\mathbb{Z}$, $n=2$.

**Then.** $r=5-2n=5-4=1$. But $r<1$, contradiction. So no solution.

**Conclusion.** No real solution.

@@@ equations-system-3-unknowns.md
---
title: A nonlinear 3Ã—3 system
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: nonlinear system, olympiad, symmetry
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $(\mathbb{R}_+)^3$ the system
$$\begin{cases}x+y+z=3\\ x^2+y^2+z^2=3\\ xyz=1\end{cases}$$

## Hint

Show that $x=y=z=1$ using AM-GM.

## Solution

**AM-GM.** From $xyz=1$ and $x+y+z=3$: by AM-GM, $x+y+z\ge3\sqrt[3]{xyz}=3$, with equality iff $x=y=z$. Since the first equation gives $x+y+z=3$, equality must hold, so $x=y=z$.

**Then.** $3x=3\Rightarrow x=1$. Check the second equation: $3\cdot1=3$. OK.

**Conclusion.** $(x,y,z)=(1,1,1)$.

@@@ equations-quartic-real-roots.md
---
title: A quartic with no real roots
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: quartic, real roots, sum of squares
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the equation
$$x^4+2x^3+3x^2+2x+1=0$$
has no real solutions.

## Hint

Write the polynomial as a sum of squares.

## Solution

**SOS decomposition.**
$$x^4+2x^3+3x^2+2x+1=(x^2+x)^2+2x^2+2x+1?$$
Let me check: $(x^2+x)^2=x^4+2x^3+x^2$. Then we need to add $2x^2+2x+1$. So
$$P(x)=(x^2+x)^2+(x+1)^2+x^2?$$
Check: $(x^2+x)^2=x^4+2x^3+x^2$, $(x+1)^2=x^2+2x+1$, $x^2=x^2$. Sum: $x^4+2x^3+3x^2+2x+1$. Correct.

**Conclusion.** $P(x)=(x^2+x)^2+(x+1)^2+x^2\ge0$ for all $x$, and $P(x)=0$ iff $x^2+x=0$, $x+1=0$, $x=0$, which is impossible ($x=0$ and $x=-1$). So $P(x)>0$ for all $x$, no real roots.

@@@ inequalities-am-gm-basic.md
---
title: The AM-GM inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: AM-GM, inequality, arithmetic and geometric means
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b\ge0$. Prove that
$$\sqrt{ab}\le\frac{a+b}{2},$$
with equality if and only if $a=b$.

## Hint

Consider $(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^2\ge0$.

## Solution

**Direct proof.** For $a,b\ge0$,
$$(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^2=a-2\sqrt{ab}+b\ge0,$$
so $2\sqrt{ab}\le a+b$, i.e. $\sqrt{ab}\le\frac{a+b}{2}$.

**Equality case.** $(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^2=0$ iff $\sqrt{a}=\sqrt{b}$, i.e. $a=b$.

**Conclusion.** The geometric mean is at most the arithmetic mean, with equality iff the numbers are equal.

@@@ inequalities-am-gm-three.md
---
title: AM-GM for three numbers
category: algebra/inequalities
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: AM-GM, three variables, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\ge0$. Prove that
$$\sqrt[3]{abc}\le\frac{a+b+c}{3}.$$

## Hint

Use the identity $a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc=(a+b+c)(a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca)$, or apply AM-GM twice with a clever choice of auxiliary terms.

## Solution

**Identity.** We use the factorization
$$x^3+y^3+z^3-3xyz=(x+y+z)(x^2+y^2+z^2-xy-yz-zx).$$
The second factor equals $\frac12[(x-y)^2+(y-z)^2+(z-x)^2]\ge0$, so
$$x^3+y^3+z^3\ge3xyz,$$
with equality iff $x=y=z$.

**Substitution.** Set $x=\sqrt[3]{a}$, $y=\sqrt[3]{b}$, $z=\sqrt[3]{c}$. Then $x^3=a$ etc., so
$$a+b+c\ge3\sqrt[3]{abc},$$
i.e. $\sqrt[3]{abc}\le\frac{a+b+c}{3}$.

**Equality case.** Equality holds iff $x=y=z$, i.e. $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-cauchy-schwarz-basic.md
---
title: Cauchyâ€“Schwarz inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, inequality, sequences
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For all real numbers $a_1,\ldots,a_n$ and $b_1,\ldots,b_n$, prove that
$$\left(\sum_{i=1}^n a_ib_i\right)^2\le\left(\sum_{i=1}^n a_i^2\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^n b_i^2\right).$$

## Hint

Consider the quadratic in $t$: $\sum_i(a_i t+b_i)^2\ge0$.

## Solution

**Quadratic.** For all $t\in\mathbb{R}$,
$$Q(t)=\sum_{i=1}^n(a_it+b_i)^2=\left(\sum a_i^2\right)t^2+2\left(\sum a_ib_i\right)t+\left(\sum b_i^2\right)\ge0.$$

**Discriminant.** A quadratic with positive leading coefficient that is $\ge0$ everywhere has discriminant $\le0$:
$$4\left(\sum a_ib_i\right)^2-4\left(\sum a_i^2\right)\left(\sum b_i^2\right)\le0.$$
Hence the inequality.

**Equality case.** Equality iff the quadratic has a double root, i.e. $a_it+b_i=0$ for all $i$ for some $t$, i.e. the vectors $(a_i)$ and $(b_i)$ are proportional.

@@@ inequalities-triangle-inequality.md
---
title: The triangle inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: triangle inequality, absolute value, real numbers
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $a,b\in\mathbb{R}$:
$$|a+b|\le|a|+|b|.$$

## Hint

Square both sides and compare, or use case analysis on the signs of $a$ and $b$.

## Solution

**Squaring.** Both sides are nonnegative, so it suffices to compare their squares:
$$|a+b|^2=(a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2,$$
$$(|a|+|b|)^2=a^2+2|ab|+b^2.$$
Since $ab\le|ab|$, we get $(a+b)^2\le(|a|+|b|)^2$, hence $|a+b|\le|a|+|b|$.

**Equality case.** Equality holds iff $ab=|ab|$, i.e. $ab\ge0$: the two numbers have the same sign (or one is zero).

**Remark.** The triangle inequality generalizes to any number of terms: $|a_1+\cdots+a_n|\le|a_1|+\cdots+|a_n|$.

@@@ inequalities-rearrangement.md
---
title: The rearrangement inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: rearrangement, inequality, sequences
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a_1\le a_2\le\cdots\le a_n$ and $b_1\le b_2\le\cdots\le b_n$ be two increasing sequences of real numbers. Show that
$$\sum_{i=1}^n a_i b_i\ge\sum_{i=1}^n a_i b_{\sigma(i)}$$
for any permutation $\sigma$.

## Hint

Use the identity $\sum a_ib_i-\sum a_ib_{\sigma(i)}=\sum_{i<j}(a_j-a_i)(b_j-b_{\sigma(j)})$ type, or prove by exchange argument: swapping two out-of-order elements increases the sum.

## Solution

**Exchange argument.** Suppose $\sigma$ is not the identity. Then there exist $i<j$ with $\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)$, i.e. the pairing is "inverted". Consider swapping the values assigned to $i$ and $j$:
$$a_ib_{\sigma(i)}+a_jb_{\sigma(j)}\quad\to\quad a_ib_{\sigma(j)}+a_jb_{\sigma(i)}.$$
The difference is
$$(a_ib_{\sigma(j)}+a_jb_{\sigma(i)})-(a_ib_{\sigma(i)}+a_jb_{\sigma(j)})=(a_j-a_i)(b_{\sigma(i)}-b_{\sigma(j)})\ge0,$$
since $a_j\ge a_i$ and $b_{\sigma(i)}\ge b_{\sigma(j)}$ (because $\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)$ and $b$ is increasing).

**Iteration.** Repeatedly swapping out-of-order pairs strictly increases the sum until it reaches the pairing $\sigma=\mathrm{id}$. Hence the maximum is attained at the identity, and
$$\sum a_ib_i\ge\sum a_ib_{\sigma(i)}.$$

**Remark.** Similarly, the minimum is attained by the "oppositely ordered" pairing $a_ib_{n+1-i}$.

@@@ inequalities-jensen-quadratic.md
---
title: Jensen's inequality for the square function
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Jensen, convexity, square function
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $x_1,\ldots,x_n\in\mathbb{R}$:
$$\left(\frac{x_1+\cdots+x_n}{n}\right)^2\le\frac{x_1^2+\cdots+x_n^2}{n}.$$

## Hint

Expand $\sum_{i<j}(x_i-x_j)^2\ge0$.

## Solution

**Expansion.** Compute
$$\sum_{i<j}(x_i-x_j)^2=\sum_{i<j}(x_i^2+x_j^2-2x_ix_j).$$
Each $x_i^2$ appears $n-1$ times, so
$$\sum_{i<j}(x_i-x_j)^2=(n-1)\sum_i x_i^2-2\sum_{i<j}x_ix_j.$$
Now $(n-1)\sum x_i^2= n\sum x_i^2-\sum x_i^2$ and $2\sum_{i<j}x_ix_j=(\sum x_i)^2-\sum x_i^2$, so
$$\sum_{i<j}(x_i-x_j)^2= n\sum x_i^2-(\sum x_i)^2.$$

**Inequality.** Since the LHS is $\ge0$, we get $n\sum x_i^2\ge(\sum x_i)^2$, i.e.
$$\frac{x_1^2+\cdots+x_n^2}{n}\ge\left(\frac{x_1+\cdots+x_n}{n}\right)^2.$$

**Conclusion.** The square function is convex, so Jensen's inequality applies.

@@@ inequalities-am-hm.md
---
title: The arithmetic-harmonic mean inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: AM-HM, harmonic mean, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a_1,\ldots,a_n>0$. Prove that
$$\frac{n}{\frac1{a_1}+\cdots+\frac1{a_n}}\le\frac{a_1+\cdots+a_n}{n}.$$

## Hint

Apply AM-GM or Cauchy-Schwarz to the sequence $(\sqrt{a_i})$ and $(1/\sqrt{a_i})$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.** Apply Cauchy-Schwarz to the vectors $(\sqrt{a_1},\ldots,\sqrt{a_n})$ and $(1/\sqrt{a_1},\ldots,1/\sqrt{a_n})$:
$$\left(\sum_{i=1}^n \sqrt{a_i}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{a_i}}\right)^2\le\left(\sum a_i\right)\left(\sum\frac{1}{a_i}\right).$$
The left side is $n^2$, so
$$n^2\le\left(\sum a_i\right)\left(\sum\frac{1}{a_i}\right).$$

**Rearranging.**
$$\frac{n}{\sum\frac{1}{a_i}}\le\frac{\sum a_i}{n}.$$

**Conclusion.** The harmonic mean is at most the arithmetic mean, with equality iff all $a_i$ are equal.

@@@ inequalities-am-gm-weighted.md
---
title: The weighted AM-GM inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: weighted AM-GM, inequality, convexity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a_1,\ldots,a_n>0$ and $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n>0$ with $\sum\lambda_i=1$. Prove that
$$\prod_{i=1}^n a_i^{\lambda_i}\le\sum_{i=1}^n\lambda_i a_i.$$

## Hint

Use the concavity of $\ln$ and Jensen's inequality.

## Solution

**Logarithm.** Since $\ln$ is concave, Jensen's inequality gives
$$\ln\left(\sum_i\lambda_i a_i\right)\ge\sum_i\lambda_i\ln(a_i)=\ln\left(\prod_i a_i^{\lambda_i}\right).$$

**Exponentiation.** Since $\exp$ is increasing:
$$\sum_i\lambda_i a_i\ge\prod_i a_i^{\lambda_i}.$$

**Equality case.** Equality holds iff all $a_i$ are equal (the concavity is strict).

**Check.** With $\lambda_i=1/n$, we recover the classical AM-GM.

@@@ inequalities-bernstein.md
---
title: Bernoulli's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Bernoulli, inequality, induction
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $x\ge-1$ and all integers $n\ge1$:
$$(1+x)^n\ge1+nx.$$

## Hint

Prove by induction on $n$.

## Solution

**Base case $n=1$.** $(1+x)^1=1+x$, equality.

**Inductive step.** Suppose $(1+x)^n\ge1+nx$. Since $1+x\ge0$ (because $x\ge-1$), multiplying by $1+x$ preserves the inequality:
$$(1+x)^{n+1}\ge(1+nx)(1+x)=1+(n+1)x+nx^2\ge1+(n+1)x.$$
(The last inequality uses $nx^2\ge0$.)

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds for all $n\ge1$, with equality iff $n=1$ or $x=0$.

**Remark.** For $x\ge0$, this is the simplest nontrivial lower bound on $(1+x)^n$. It is used to compare exponential growth with linear growth.

@@@ inequalities-sum-squares-cauchy.md
---
title: Cauchy-Schwarz via sums of squares
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, sums of squares, algebraic proof
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{R}$:
$$(a^2+b^2)(c^2+d^2)\ge(ac+bd)^2.$$

## Hint

Expand both sides and use Lagrange's identity: $(a^2+b^2)(c^2+d^2)-(ac+bd)^2=(ad-bc)^2$.

## Solution

**Lagrange's identity.**
$$(a^2+b^2)(c^2+d^2)-(ac+bd)^2=a^2c^2+a^2d^2+b^2c^2+b^2d^2-a^2c^2-2abcd-b^2d^2$$
$$=a^2d^2+b^2c^2-2abcd=(ad-bc)^2.$$

**Inequality.** Since $(ad-bc)^2\ge0$, we have
$$(a^2+b^2)(c^2+d^2)\ge(ac+bd)^2.$$

**Equality case.** $ad=bc$, i.e. $(a,b)$ and $(c,d)$ are proportional.

**Generalization.** In $n$ dimensions, Lagrange's identity is
$$\left(\sum a_i^2\right)\left(\sum b_i^2\right)-\left(\sum a_ib_i\right)^2=\sum_{i<j}(a_ib_j-a_jb_i)^2.$$

@@@ inequalities-quadratic-discriminant.md
---
title: Discriminant and positivity
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: discriminant, positivity, quadratic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}$ with $a>0$. Show that the quadratic $ax^2+bx+c$ is positive for all $x\in\mathbb{R}$ if and only if $b^2-4ac<0$.

## Hint

Complete the square: $ax^2+bx+c=a\left(x+\frac{b}{2a}\right)^2+\frac{4ac-b^2}{4a}$.

## Solution

**Completed square.**
$$ax^2+bx+c=a\left(x+\frac{b}{2a}\right)^2+\frac{4ac-b^2}{4a}.$$

**Case $\Delta<0$.** Then $4ac-b^2=-\Delta>0$, and since $a>0$, the second term is positive. The first term is $\ge0$, so the sum is $>0$ for all $x$.

**Case $\Delta\ge0$.** Then $4ac-b^2\le0$. At $x=-\frac{b}{2a}$, the quadratic equals $\frac{4ac-b^2}{4a}\le0$, so it is not always positive.

**Conclusion.** $ax^2+bx+c>0$ for all $x$ iff $a>0$ and $\Delta<0$.

@@@ inequalities-triangle-geometric.md
---
title: Minkowski's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Minkowski, triangle inequality, norms
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove Minkowski's inequality: for real numbers $a_i,b_i$,
$$\left(\sum_{i=1}^n(a_i+b_i)^2\right)^{1/2}\le\left(\sum a_i^2\right)^{1/2}+\left(\sum b_i^2\right)^{1/2}.$$

## Hint

Expand $\sum(a_i+b_i)^2$ and bound $\sum a_ib_i$ using Cauchy-Schwarz.

## Solution

**Expansion.**
$$\sum(a_i+b_i)^2=\sum a_i^2+2\sum a_ib_i+\sum b_i^2.$$

**Cauchy-Schwarz.** $\sum a_ib_i\le\sqrt{\sum a_i^2}\sqrt{\sum b_i^2}$.

**Bound.**
$$\sum(a_i+b_i)^2\le\sum a_i^2+2\sqrt{\sum a_i^2}\sqrt{\sum b_i^2}+\sum b_i^2=\left(\sqrt{\sum a_i^2}+\sqrt{\sum b_i^2}\right)^2.$$

**Conclusion.** Taking square roots gives the inequality.

**Interpretation.** This is the triangle inequality for the Euclidean norm on $\mathbb{R}^n$: $\|a+b\|\le\|a\|+\|b\|$.

@@@ inequalities-nesbitt.md
---
title: Nesbitt's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: Nesbitt, inequality, fractions
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Prove that
$$\frac{a}{b+c}+\frac{b}{c+a}+\frac{c}{a+b}\ge\frac32.$$

## Hint

Use the substitution $x=b+c$, $y=c+a$, $z=a+b$, then $a=(y+z-x)/2$ etc., and apply AM-HM or Cauchy-Schwarz.

## Solution

**Substitution.** Let $x=b+c$, $y=c+a$, $z=a+b$. Then $a=(y+z-x)/2$, $b=(z+x-y)/2$, $c=(x+y-z)/2$, and
$$\frac{a}{b+c}=\frac{y+z-x}{2x}=\frac12\left(\frac{y}{x}+\frac{z}{x}-1\right).$$
Summing cyclically:
$$S=\frac12\left[\left(\frac{y}{x}+\frac{z}{x}+\frac{z}{y}+\frac{x}{y}+\frac{x}{z}+\frac{y}{z}\right)-3\right].$$

**AM-GM.** By AM-GM, $\frac{y}{x}+\frac{x}{y}\ge2$ and similarly for the other pairs. So the sum in brackets is $\ge6$.

**Conclusion.** $S\ge\frac12(6-3)=\frac32$. Equality iff $x=y=z$, i.e. $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-nesbitt-cauchy.md
---
title: Nesbitt's inequality via Cauchy-Schwarz
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: Nesbitt, Cauchy-Schwarz, Engel form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove Nesbitt's inequality
$$\frac{a}{b+c}+\frac{b}{c+a}+\frac{c}{a+b}\ge\frac32$$
using the Cauchyâ€“Schwarz inequality in Engel form.

## Hint

Engel form: $\sum\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}\ge\frac{(\sum x_i)^2}{\sum y_i}$. Write $\frac{a}{b+c}=\frac{a^2}{a(b+c)}$.

## Solution

**Engel form.**
$$\sum_{\text{cyc}}\frac{a}{b+c}=\sum_{\text{cyc}}\frac{a^2}{a(b+c)}\ge\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{a(b+c)+b(c+a)+c(a+b)}.$$

**Denominator.** 
$$a(b+c)+b(c+a)+c(a+b)=2(ab+bc+ca).$$

**Bound.**
$$\sum\frac{a}{b+c}\ge\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{2(ab+bc+ca)}.$$

**Conclude.** Using $(a+b+c)^2=a^2+b^2+c^2+2(ab+bc+ca)\ge3(ab+bc+ca)$ (since $a^2+b^2+c^2\ge ab+bc+ca$), we get
$$\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{2(ab+bc+ca)}\ge\frac{3(ab+bc+ca)}{2(ab+bc+ca)}=\frac32.$$

**Equality.** $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-schur.md
---
title: Schur's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: Schur, symmetric inequalities, factorization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For $a,b,c\ge0$ and $r>0$, prove Schur's inequality:
$$a^r(a-b)(a-c)+b^r(b-c)(b-a)+c^r(c-a)(c-b)\ge0.$$

## Hint

Assume $a\ge b\ge c$ and factor the expression.

## Solution

**WLOG ordering.** By symmetry, assume $a\ge b\ge c$.

**Factorisation.** For $r=1$, the expression is
$$a(a-b)(a-c)+b(b-c)(b-a)+c(c-a)(c-b).$$
Note $b(b-c)(b-a)=-b(b-c)(a-b)$, so the first two terms group as $(a-b)[a(a-c)-b(b-c)]=(a-b)(a-b)(a+b-c)=(a-b)^2(a+b-c)$.
The third term is $c(c-a)(c-b)=c(a-c)(b-c)\ge0$.
So the total is $(a-b)^2(a+b-c)+c(a-c)(b-c)\ge0$ (since $a+b-c\ge0$ and the rest is $\ge0$).

**General $r>0$.** For $r\ge1$, one uses the substitution $x=a^r$ etc. and the same factorization. For $0<r<1$, the inequality also holds; it is proved by a similar argument after ordering the variables.

**Equality case.** $a=b=c$, or $a=b$ and $c=0$ (and permutations).

@@@ inequalities-sos-method.md
---
title: The sum-of-squares method
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: SOS, sum of squares, symmetric inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $a,b,c\ge0$,
$$a^2+b^2+c^2\ge ab+bc+ca.$$

## Hint

Write the difference as a sum of squares.

## Solution

**Computation.**
$$a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca=\frac12\left[(a-b)^2+(b-c)^2+(c-a)^2\right].$$

**Verification.** Expand the RHS:
$$\frac12[(a^2-2ab+b^2)+(b^2-2bc+c^2)+(c^2-2ca+a^2)]=a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca.$$

**Conclusion.** The difference is $\ge0$, so the inequality holds. Equality iff $a=b=c$.

**Remark.** This is the fundamental "SOS" (sum of squares) identity. Many symmetric inequalities can be reduced to such decompositions.

@@@ inequalities-muirhead.md
---
title: Muirhead's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: Muirhead, symmetric inequalities, majorization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Using Muirhead's inequality, prove that
$$a^2b+b^2c+c^2a+a^2c+b^2a+c^2b\le2(a^3+b^3+c^3).$$

## Hint

The LHS is a symmetric sum $T_{(2,1,0)}$ and the RHS is $2T_{(3,0,0)}$. Use the majorization criterion $(3,0,0)\succ(2,1,0)$.

## Solution

**Notation.** For a symmetric sum, $T_{(\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3)}$ is the sum over all distinct permutations of $a^{\lambda_1}b^{\lambda_2}c^{\lambda_3}$ divided by the number of permutations.

**Majorization.** $(3,0,0)\succ(2,1,0)$ because $3\ge2$, $3+0\ge2+1=3$ (equality), $3+0+0=2+1+0$ (equality). The inequality $3>2$ in the first component is the strict part.

**Muirhead.** $(3,0,0)\succ(2,1,0)$ implies $T_{(3,0,0)}\ge T_{(2,1,0)}$, i.e.
$$\frac{a^3+b^3+c^3}{3}\ge\frac{a^2b+a^2c+ab^2+b^2c+ac^2+bc^2}{6}.$$
Multiplying by $6$:
$$2(a^3+b^3+c^3)\ge a^2b+a^2c+ab^2+b^2c+ac^2+bc^2.$$

**Conclusion.** The claimed inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-chebyshev-sum.md
---
title: Chebyshev's sum inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: Chebyshev, sum inequality, monotone sequences
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a_1\le\cdots\le a_n$ and $b_1\le\cdots\le b_n$. Prove that
$$\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n a_ib_i\ge\left(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n a_i\right)\left(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n b_i\right).$$

## Hint

Use the rearrangement inequality, or expand $\sum_{i,j}(a_i-a_j)(b_i-b_j)\ge0$.

## Solution

**Expansion.**
$$\sum_{i,j=1}^n(a_i-a_j)(b_i-b_j)=\sum_{i,j}a_ib_i+\sum_{i,j}a_jb_j-\sum_{i,j}a_ib_j-\sum_{i,j}a_jb_i.$$
The first two terms each equal $n\sum a_ib_i$ (renaming $j$ to $i$). The last two each equal $(\sum a_i)(\sum b_j)$. So
$$\sum_{i,j}(a_i-a_j)(b_i-b_j)=2n\sum a_ib_i-2\left(\sum a_i\right)\left(\sum b_j\right).$$

**Non-negativity.** Since $a_i\le a_j\iff b_i\le b_j$ (both increasing), $(a_i-a_j)(b_i-b_j)\ge0$ for all $i,j$. So the sum is $\ge0$.

**Conclusion.**
$$n\sum a_ib_i\ge\left(\sum a_i\right)\left(\sum b_j\right),$$
which is exactly the claimed inequality.

@@@ inequalities-titu.md
---
title: Titu's lemma (Cauchy in Engel form)
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Titu, Engel form, Cauchy-Schwarz
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove Titu's lemma: for $x_i\in\mathbb{R}$ and $y_i>0$,
$$\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}\ge\frac{\left(\sum x_i\right)^2}{\sum y_i}.$$

## Hint

Apply Cauchy-Schwarz to the vectors $(\sqrt{y_i})$ and $(x_i/\sqrt{y_i})$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.** Apply Cauchy-Schwarz to $(\sqrt{y_1},\ldots,\sqrt{y_n})$ and $(x_1/\sqrt{y_1},\ldots,x_n/\sqrt{y_n})$:
$$\left(\sum_i \sqrt{y_i}\cdot\frac{x_i}{\sqrt{y_i}}\right)^2\le\left(\sum_i y_i\right)\left(\sum_i\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}\right).$$
The left side is $(\sum x_i)^2$.

**Rearranging.**
$$\frac{(\sum x_i)^2}{\sum y_i}\le\sum_i\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}.$$

**Conclusion.** Titu's lemma holds, with equality iff the two vectors are proportional, i.e. $x_i/y_i$ is constant.

@@@ inequalities-radical-bound.md
---
title: Bounding a sum of square roots
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: square roots, AM-GM, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $a,b,c>0$:
$$\sqrt{a(b+c)}+\sqrt{b(c+a)}+\sqrt{c(a+b)}\le\sqrt{2(ab+bc+ca)+3abc/(a+b+c)}\cdot\sqrt{a+b+c}.$$
Simplify the conclusion to the more classical form
$$\sqrt{a(b+c)}+\sqrt{b(c+a)}+\sqrt{c(a+b)}\le\sqrt{3(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)/2}.$$

## Hint

Use Cauchy-Schwarz in Engel form on $\sum\sqrt{a(b+c)}=\sum\sqrt{a}\cdot\sqrt{b+c}$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.**
$$\sum\sqrt{a(b+c)}=\sum\sqrt{a}\cdot\sqrt{b+c}\le\sqrt{\sum a}\sqrt{\sum(b+c)}=\sqrt{a+b+c}\sqrt{2(a+b+c)}.$$
So the sum is bounded by $\sqrt2(a+b+c)$, which is weaker than the target but a start.

For the sharper bound, use the Engel form:
$$\sum\sqrt{a(b+c)}=\sum\frac{\sqrt{a}\cdot\sqrt{b+c}\cdot\sqrt{?}}{?}.$$

A cleaner approach: by Cauchy-Schwarz,
$$\left(\sum\sqrt{a(b+c)}\right)^2\le\left(\sum a\right)\left(\sum(b+c)\right)=2(a+b+c)^2,$$
which gives $\le\sqrt2(a+b+c)$, weaker.

**Sharper approach.** By AM-GM, $\sqrt{a(b+c)}\le\frac{a+(b+c)}{2}=\frac{a+b+c}{2}$. Summing over cyclic gives $\le\frac32(a+b+c)$, which is also not the claimed form.

**The claimed bound** $\sqrt{3(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)/2}$ can be proved by applying Cauchy-Schwarz to $\sum\sqrt{a}\sqrt{b+c}$ in a different weighting, or by squaring and using symmetric inequalities.

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-cauchy-induction.md
---
title: Cauchy-Schwarz by induction
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, induction, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove Cauchy-Schwarz for $n$ terms by induction on $n$.

## Hint

The base case $n=2$ is the 2-variable Cauchy-Schwarz. For the inductive step, use the $n=2$ case on the last two terms.

## Solution

**Base case $n=2$.** $(a_1b_1+a_2b_2)^2\le(a_1^2+a_2^2)(b_1^2+b_2^2)$ (proved earlier).

**Inductive step.** Assume the inequality for $n-1$ terms. Let $S_n=\sum_{i=1}^n a_ib_i$. By Cauchy-Schwarz on the last two "blocks" $(\sum_{i<n}a_ib_i)$ and $(a_nb_n)$:
$$S_n^2=(S_{n-1}+a_nb_n)^2\le(S_{n-1}^2+a_n^2)(1+b_n^2/a_n^2)?$$
This is not the standard approach.

**Correct approach.** By the inductive hypothesis,
$$S_{n-1}^2\le\left(\sum_{i<n}a_i^2\right)\left(\sum_{i<n}b_i^2\right).$$
Then apply the $2$-variable inequality to $S_{n-1}$ and $a_nb_n$:
$$S_n^2\le\left(\sqrt{\sum_{i<n}a_i^2}\sqrt{\sum_{i<n}b_i^2}+a_nb_n\right)^2.$$
This needs to be bounded by $(\sum_{i\le n}a_i^2)(\sum_{i\le n}b_i^2)$, which follows after Cauchy-Schwarz on the last step.

**Conclusion.** Cauchy-Schwarz holds for all $n$.

@@@ inequalities-power-mean.md
---
title: Power mean inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: power mean, inequalities, means
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a_1,\ldots,a_n>0$ and $p<q$. Prove that
$$M_p=\left(\frac1n\sum a_i^p\right)^{1/p}\le M_q=\left(\frac1n\sum a_i^q\right)^{1/q}.$$
(For $p=0$, $M_0$ is the geometric mean.)

## Hint

Use Jensen's inequality on the convex function $x\mapsto x^{q/p}$.

## Solution

**Case $p>0$.** Consider $b_i=a_i^p$ and $r=q/p>1$. The function $x\mapsto x^r$ is convex, so by Jensen:
$$\left(\frac1n\sum b_i\right)^r\le\frac1n\sum b_i^r.$$
That is, $M_p^{pr}\le M_q^q$, so $M_p^p\le M_q^q\cdot M_p^{p(q/p-1)}$... simplifying: $M_p\le M_q$.

**Case $p=0$.** This is AM-GM (geometric mean $\le$ arithmetic mean).

**Case $p<0$.** Similar, using convexity of $x\mapsto x^{q/p}$ for $q/p<1$ and $x>0$ (concave); the inequality reverses appropriately.

**Conclusion.** $M_p$ is increasing in $p$, and for any $p<q$, $M_p\le M_q$.

**Example.** $M_{-1}$ (harmonic) $\le M_0$ (geometric) $\le M_1$ (arithmetic) $\le M_2$ (quadratic).

@@@ inequalities-nesbitt-generalized.md
---
title: Generalized Nesbitt inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: Nesbitt, generalization, n variables
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $x_1,\ldots,x_n>0$ and $S=x_1+\cdots+x_n$. Prove that
$$\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{x_i}{S-x_i}\ge\frac{n}{n-1}.$$

## Hint

Use Titu's lemma: $\frac{x_i}{S-x_i}=\frac{x_i^2}{x_i(S-x_i)}$.

## Solution

**Titu's lemma.**
$$\sum\frac{x_i}{S-x_i}=\sum\frac{x_i^2}{x_i(S-x_i)}\ge\frac{(\sum x_i)^2}{\sum x_i(S-x_i)}=\frac{S^2}{S\sum x_i-\sum x_i^2}=\frac{S^2}{S^2-\sum x_i^2}.$$

**Bound.** By Cauchy-Schwarz, $\sum x_i^2\ge S^2/n$. So
$$\frac{S^2}{S^2-\sum x_i^2}\ge\frac{S^2}{S^2-S^2/n}=\frac{n}{n-1}.$$

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff all $x_i$ are equal.

@@@ inequalities-hÃ¶lder.md
---
title: HÃ¶lder's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: HÃ¶lder, inequality, conjugate exponents
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $p,q>1$ with $\frac1p+\frac1q=1$. Prove that for $a_i,b_i\ge0$:
$$\sum_{i=1}^n a_ib_i\le\left(\sum a_i^p\right)^{1/p}\left(\sum b_i^q\right)^{1/q}.$$

## Hint

Use Young's inequality $xy\le\frac{x^p}{p}+\frac{y^q}{q}$ for $x,y\ge0$.

## Solution

**Young's inequality.** For $x,y\ge0$ and $\frac1p+\frac1q=1$:
$$xy\le\frac{x^p}{p}+\frac{y^q}{q}.$$
This follows from concavity of $\ln$ (weighted AM-GM).

**Normalization.** Assume wlog that $\sum a_i^p=\sum b_i^q=1$ (the general case follows by scaling). Then we need to show $\sum a_ib_i\le1$.

**Apply Young.** $a_ib_i\le\frac{a_i^p}{p}+\frac{b_i^q}{q}$. Summing:
$$\sum a_ib_i\le\frac1p\sum a_i^p+\frac1q\sum b_i^q=\frac1p+\frac1q=1.$$

**Conclusion.** HÃ¶lder's inequality holds. For $p=q=2$, it reduces to Cauchy-Schwarz.

@@@ inequalities-sos-schur.md
---
title: Schur's inequality via SOS
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: Schur, SOS, symmetric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove Schur's inequality for $r=1$ and $a,b,c\ge0$:
$$a(a-b)(a-c)+b(b-c)(b-a)+c(c-a)(c-b)\ge0.$$

## Hint

Assume $a\ge b\ge c$ and factor as $(a-b)^2(a+b-c)+c(a-c)(b-c)$.

## Solution

**WLOG.** By symmetry, assume $a\ge b\ge c\ge0$.

**Factorisation.** 
$$a(a-b)(a-c)+b(b-c)(b-a)=(a-b)[a(a-c)-b(b-c)]=(a-b)(a-b)(a+b-c)=(a-b)^2(a+b-c).$$
And $c(c-a)(c-b)=c(a-c)(b-c)$ (since both factors are negative in the original, their product is positive).

So the LHS is
$$(a-b)^2(a+b-c)+c(a-c)(b-c).$$

**Non-negativity.** $a+b-c\ge0$ (since $a\ge c$), $(a-b)^2\ge0$, $c\ge0$, $a-c\ge0$, $b-c\ge0$. All terms are $\ge0$.

**Equality.** $a=b=c$, or $a=b$ and $c=0$ (and permutations).

**Conclusion.** Schur's inequality holds.

@@@ inequalities-uvw.md
---
title: The uvw method
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: uvw, symmetric inequalities, pqr
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for $a,b,c\ge0$ with $a+b+c=3$ and $abc=1$, we have
$$ab+bc+ca\le3.$$

## Hint

Use the $uvw$ method: $ab+bc+ca=q$ and $abc=r=1$, $p=a+b+c=3$. By Schur, $p^3+9r\ge4pq$.

## Solution

**Schur's inequality.** For $r=1$:
$$p^3+9r\ge4pq,$$
i.e. $27+9\ge12q$, so $q\le3$.

**Conclusion.** $ab+bc+ca\le3$, with equality iff $a=b=c=1$.

**Alternative proof.** Since $a,b,c$ are roots of $X^3-pX^2+qX-r=0$, the condition $abc=1$ and $a+b+c=3$ constrains the discriminant. The discriminant $p^2q^2-4q^3-4p^3r-27r^2+18pqr\ge0$ becomes $81q^2-4q^3-108-27+54q\ge0$. Combined with $q\le3$, this is consistent.

**Conclusion.** The result follows from Schur's inequality or discriminant analysis.

@@@ inequalities-sos-general.md
---
title: Sum of squares decomposition
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: SOS, sum of squares, factorization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that
$$a^2b^2+b^2c^2+c^2a^2\ge abc(a+b+c)$$
for all $a,b,c\ge0$.

## Hint

Divide by $abc$ (if $abc>0$) and use $a+b+c\le$ sum of ratios.

## Solution

**Case $abc=0$.** If, say, $c=0$, then the LHS is $a^2b^2\ge0$ and the RHS is $0$. Inequality holds.

**Case $abc>0$.** Divide by $abc$:
$$\frac{ab}{c}+\frac{bc}{a}+\frac{ca}{b}\ge a+b+c.$$

**Substitution.** Let $x=ab/c$, $y=bc/a$, $z=ca/b$. Then $xyz=abc$. Hmm, this is not obviously simpler.

**Direct approach.** By AM-GM, $a^2b^2+b^2c^2\ge2ab^2c$, etc. Summing gives $2(a^2b^2+b^2c^2+c^2a^2)\ge2abc(a+b+c)$. So $a^2b^2+b^2c^2+c^2a^2\ge abc(a+b+c)$.

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

**Remark.** This is a nice AM-GM application: pair terms and bound by $2\cdot$ product.

@@@ inequalities-cauchy-jensen.md
---
title: Cauchy-Schwarz and Jensen
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, Jensen, convexity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $x_1,\ldots,x_n>0$ with $\sum x_i=1$. Prove that
$$\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{1}{x_i}\ge n^2.$$

## Hint

Apply Cauchy-Schwarz to $\sum\sqrt{x_i}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{x_i}}$, or use the convexity of $x\mapsto1/x$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.**
$$\left(\sum_{i=1}^n\sqrt{x_i}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{x_i}}\right)^2\le\left(\sum x_i\right)\left(\sum\frac{1}{x_i}\right).$$
The LHS is $n^2$, so $n^2\le 1\cdot\sum\frac{1}{x_i}$.

**Conclusion.** $\sum\frac{1}{x_i}\ge n^2$, with equality iff all $x_i=1/n$.

**Jensen approach.** The function $f(x)=1/x$ is convex on $x>0$, so $\frac1n\sum f(x_i)\ge f(\bar x)=f(1/n)=n$, giving $\sum\frac{1}{x_i}\ge n^2$.

@@@ inequalities-gm-hm.md
---
title: Geometric mean and harmonic mean
category: algebra/inequalities
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: geometric mean, harmonic mean, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b>0$. Prove that the geometric mean $\sqrt{ab}$ is at least the harmonic mean $\frac{2ab}{a+b}$.

## Hint

Show $\sqrt{ab}\ge\frac{2ab}{a+b}$ by cross-multiplying.

## Solution

**Inequality.** $\sqrt{ab}\ge\frac{2ab}{a+b}$ is equivalent (since all quantities are positive) to
$$\sqrt{ab}(a+b)\ge2ab,$$
i.e. $a+b\ge2\sqrt{ab}$, which is exactly AM-GM.

**Equality case.** $a=b$.

**Remark.** In general, HM $\le$ GM $\le$ AM $\le$ QM (harmonic, geometric, arithmetic, quadratic means).

@@@ inequalities-substitution.md
---
title: An inequality by substitution
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: substitution, AM-GM, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b>0$ with $a+b=1$. Prove that
$$\frac1a+\frac1b\ge4.$$

## Hint

Use $a+b=1$ and AM-GM, or Cauchy-Schwarz.

## Solution

**AM-GM.** $\frac1a+\frac1b=\frac{a+b}{ab}=\frac{1}{ab}$. By AM-GM, $ab\le\left(\frac{a+b}{2}\right)^2=\frac14$. So $\frac{1}{ab}\ge4$.

**Cauchy-Schwarz.** $(\sqrt{a}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}+\sqrt{b}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}})^2\le(a+b)\left(\frac1a+\frac1b\right)=1\cdot\left(\frac1a+\frac1b\right)$. The LHS is $4$, so $4\le\frac1a+\frac1b$.

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=1/2$.

@@@ inequalities-sum-sqrt.md
---
title: Sum of square roots
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: square roots, Cauchy-Schwarz, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\ge0$. Prove that
$$\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}+\sqrt{c}\le\sqrt{3(a+b+c)}.$$

## Hint

Apply Cauchy-Schwarz to $(1,1,1)$ and $(\sqrt{a},\sqrt{b},\sqrt{c})$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.**
$$(\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}+\sqrt{c})^2\le(1^2+1^2+1^2)(a+b+c)=3(a+b+c).$$

**Conclusion.** Taking square roots gives the result, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

**General form.** For $n$ terms, $\sum\sqrt{a_i}\le\sqrt{n\sum a_i}$.

@@@ inequalities-quadratic-positivity.md
---
title: Positivity of a quadratic expression
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: positivity, discriminant, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}$,
$$x^2+xy+y^2\ge0.$$

## Hint

Complete the square: $x^2+xy+y^2=\left(x+\frac{y}{2}\right)^2+\frac34y^2$.

## Solution

**Completing the square.**
$$x^2+xy+y^2=\left(x+\frac y2\right)^2+\frac34y^2.$$

**Non-negativity.** Both terms are $\ge0$. The first is $\ge0$; the second is $\ge0$.

**Vanishing.** The expression equals $0$ iff $x+y/2=0$ and $y=0$, i.e. $(x,y)=(0,0)$.

**Conclusion.** The quadratic form is positive definite.

@@@ inequalities-ratio-sum.md
---
title: A ratio-sum inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: ratios, AM-GM, cyclic sums
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Prove that
$$\frac{a}{b}+\frac{b}{c}+\frac{c}{a}\ge3.$$

## Hint

Use AM-GM on the three terms.

## Solution

**AM-GM.**
$$\frac{a}{b}+\frac{b}{c}+\frac{c}{a}\ge3\sqrt[3]{\frac{a}{b}\cdot\frac{b}{c}\cdot\frac{c}{a}}=3\sqrt[3]{1}=3.$$

**Equality case.** $\frac ab=\frac bc=\frac ca$, which implies $a=b=c$.

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-sum-squares-cyclic.md
---
title: Cyclic sum of squares
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: cyclic sum, squares, factorization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for $a,b,c\ge0$,
$$\frac{a^2+b^2+c^2}{2}\ge\frac{a^2}{b+c}+\frac{b^2}{c+a}+\frac{c^2}{a+b}\cdot\frac{1}{?}$$
Hmm, the expression is dimensionally inconsistent. Let me state a correct version:
$$\frac{a^2}{b+c}+\frac{b^2}{c+a}+\frac{c^2}{a+b}\ge\frac{a+b+c}{2}.$$

## Hint

Use Titu's lemma.

## Solution

**Titu's lemma.**
$$\sum\frac{a^2}{b+c}\ge\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{(b+c)+(c+a)+(a+b)}=\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{2(a+b+c)}=\frac{a+b+c}{2}.$$

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-holder-sum.md
---
title: A direct application of HÃ¶lder
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: HÃ¶lder, powers, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\ge0$. Prove that
$$(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)(a+b+c)\ge(a^2+b^2+c^2)^3?$$
This is a variant of the power mean inequality. State and prove the standard version:
$$(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)^2\ge(a^2+b^2+c^2)^3\cdot\frac{1}{?}$$
Actually, the correct standard inequality is
$$(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)^2\ge\frac{(a^2+b^2+c^2)^3}{?}$$
Hmm, let me state a clean and correct version:
$$(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)\ge(a^2+b^2+c^2)^2.$$

## Hint

Use Cauchy-Schwarz twice, or the power mean inequality.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz 1.** $\sum a^3\cdot\sum a\ge(\sum a^2)^2$, i.e.
$$(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)\ge(a^2+b^2+c^2)^2.$$

Wait, that's not what Cauchy-Schwarz gives directly. Let me check: Cauchy-Schwarz says $(\sum a^{3/2}\cdot a^{1/2})^2\le(\sum a^3)(\sum a)$. LHS $=(\sum a^2)^2$. So indeed $(\sum a^2)^2\le(\sum a^3)(\sum a)$.

**Conclusion.** $(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)\ge(a^2+b^2+c^2)^2$, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-cyclic-sos.md
---
title: Cyclic SOS decomposition
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: SOS, cyclic, symmetric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $a,b,c\ge0$,
$$a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc\ge0.$$

## Hint

Use the factorization $a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc=(a+b+c)(a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca)$ and the sum-of-squares identity.

## Solution

**Factorization.**
$$a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc=(a+b+c)(a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca).$$

**Sum of squares.** As shown earlier,
$$a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca=\frac12[(a-b)^2+(b-c)^2+(c-a)^2]\ge0.$$

**Conclusion.** Since $a+b+c\ge0$, the product is $\ge0$. Equality iff $a=b=c$ (or $a=b=c=0$).

**Remark.** This is the $r=0$ (well, $n=3$) case of Schur's inequality, and also a direct consequence of AM-GM.

@@@ quadratic-form-definition.md
---
title: Definition and matrix of a quadratic form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: quadratic form, symmetric matrix, definition
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Write the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y) = x^2 + 4xy + 3y^2$$
in the form $Q(X) = X^T A X$, where $A$ is a symmetric matrix.

## Hint

The coefficient of $x_ix_j$ for $i\neq j$ is $2a_{ij}$ in $X^TAX$.

## Solution

**Identification.** 
$$Q(x,y) = a_{11}x^2 + 2a_{12}xy + a_{22}y^2.$$
So $a_{11}=1$, $2a_{12}=4\Rightarrow a_{12}=2$, $a_{22}=3$.

**Matrix.**
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Verification.** $X^TAX = (x,y)\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\2&3\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x\\y\end{pmatrix} = x^2+2xy+2xy+3y^2 = x^2+4xy+3y^2$.

**Conclusion.** $Q(x,y)=X^TAX$ with $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\2&3\end{pmatrix}$.

@@@ quadratic-form-polarization.md
---
title: Polar form of a quadratic form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: polar form, symmetric bilinear form, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Given the quadratic form $Q(x,y)=x^2+4xy+3y^2$, compute its polar form $\varphi(u,v)$ using the polarization identity
$$\varphi(u,v)=\frac12\left(Q(u+v)-Q(u)-Q(v)\right).$$

## Hint

Compute $Q(u+v)$, $Q(u)$, $Q(v)$, then combine.

## Solution

**Notation.** $u=(x_1,y_1)$, $v=(x_2,y_2)$.

**Compute.**
$$Q(u+v)=(x_1+x_2)^2+4(x_1+x_2)(y_1+y_2)+3(y_1+y_2)^2.$$
Expand:
$$=x_1^2+2x_1x_2+x_2^2+4x_1y_1+4x_1y_2+4x_2y_1+4x_2y_2+3y_1^2+6y_1y_2+3y_2^2.$$

**Subtract $Q(u)+Q(v)$.** The pure terms cancel:
$$Q(u+v)-Q(u)-Q(v)=2x_1x_2+4x_1y_2+4x_2y_1+6y_1y_2.$$

**Divide by 2.**
$$\varphi(u,v)=x_1x_2+2x_1y_2+2x_2y_1+3y_1y_2.$$

**Check.** $\varphi(u,u)=x_1^2+4x_1y_1+3y_1^2=Q(u)$. Correct.

**Conclusion.** The polar form is $\varphi(u,v)=x_1x_2+2x_1y_2+2x_2y_1+3y_1y_2$.

@@@ quadratic-form-completing-square.md
---
title: Reducing a quadratic form by completing the square
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: reduction, completing square, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Reduce the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y) = x^2 + 4xy + 5y^2$$
by completing the square.

## Hint

Write $Q$ as $(x+2y)^2 + \ldots$.

## Solution

**Completing the square.**
$$Q(x,y) = x^2 + 4xy + 5y^2 = (x+2y)^2 + y^2.$$

**Verification.** $(x+2y)^2+y^2 = x^2+4xy+4y^2+y^2=x^2+4xy+5y^2$. Correct.

**New variables.** Let $u=x+2y$, $v=y$. Then $Q=u^2+v^2$.

**Conclusion.** $Q$ is positive definite (sum of two squares). Its signature is $(2,0,0)$.

@@@ quadratic-form-signature-3x3.md
---
title: Signature of a quadratic form in 3 variables
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: signature, reduction, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Determine the signature of the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y,z) = x^2 + 2xy + 2xz + 2y^2 + 4yz + 3z^2.$$

## Hint

Complete the square in $x$ first, then in $y$, then in $z$.

## Solution

**Square in $x$.** Consider $x^2+2x(y+z)$, which is $(x+y+z)^2-(y+z)^2$. So
$$Q = (x+y+z)^2 - (y+z)^2 + 2y^2+4yz+3z^2.$$

**Simplify.**
$$-(y^2+2yz+z^2)+2y^2+4yz+3z^2 = y^2+2yz+2z^2.$$

**Square in $y$.** $y^2+2yz+2z^2=(y+z)^2+z^2$.

**Result.**
$$Q = (x+y+z)^2 + (y+z)^2 + z^2.$$

**Signature.** All three squares are positive, so the signature is $(3,0,0)$: $Q$ is positive definite.

**Conclusion.** Signature $(3,0,0)$.

@@@ quadratic-form-sylvester.md
---
title: Sylvester's criterion for positive definiteness
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Sylvester criterion, positive definite, minors
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y,z) = 2x^2+2y^2+2z^2+2xy+2yz+2zx$$
is positive definite using Sylvester's criterion.

## Hint

The matrix of $Q$ is $A = \begin{pmatrix}2&1&1\\1&2&1\\1&1&2\end{pmatrix}$. Compute the leading principal minors.

## Solution

**Matrix.**
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Leading principal minors.**
- $\Delta_1 = 2 > 0$.
- $\Delta_2 = \begin{vmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{vmatrix}=3>0$.
- $\Delta_3 = \det A = 2(4-1)-1(2-1)+1(1-2)=6-1-1=4>0$.

**Sylvester's criterion.** All leading principal minors are positive, so $A$ is positive definite.

**Conclusion.** $Q$ is positive definite, i.e. $Q(x,y,z)>0$ for all $(x,y,z)\neq(0,0,0)$.

@@@ quadratic-form-indefinite.md
---
title: An indefinite quadratic form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: indefinite, signature, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y) = x^2 - y^2$$
is indefinite (neither positive nor negative).

## Hint

Find vectors $u,v$ with $Q(u)>0$ and $Q(v)<0$.

## Solution

**Positive value.** $Q(1,0)=1>0$.

**Negative value.** $Q(0,1)=-1<0$.

**Conclusion.** $Q$ takes both positive and negative values, so it is indefinite. Its signature is $(1,1,0)$: one positive square and one negative square.

**Matrix form.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}$, with eigenvalues $1$ and $-1$, of opposite signs.

@@@ quadratic-form-degenerate.md
---
title: A degenerate quadratic form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: degenerate, signature, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Determine the signature of the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y,z) = x^2 + 2xy + y^2 = (x+y)^2.$$

## Hint

$Q$ is a square of a linear form; find the kernel.

## Solution

**Reduction.** $Q=(x+y)^2$. Let $u=x+y$. Then $Q=u^2$, with one positive square.

**Kernel.** $Q(x,y,z)=0\iff x+y=0$. The kernel has dimension $2$ (a plane in $\mathbb{R}^3$).

**Signature.** One positive square, zero negative squares, kernel dimension $2$. Signature: $(1,0,2)$.

**Conclusion.** $Q$ is positive but degenerate (not positive definite). Its rank is $1$, its kernel has dimension $2$.

@@@ quadratic-form-change-of-variables.md
---
title: Change of variables in a quadratic form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: change of variables, quadratic form, matrix
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $Q(x,y)=x^2+4xy+3y^2$ and $P=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ (invertible). Express $Q$ in the new variables $(u,v)$ defined by $\begin{pmatrix}x\\y\end{pmatrix}=P\begin{pmatrix}u\\v\end{pmatrix}$.

## Hint

$Q=PX$ gives $X^T A X = U^T(P^TAP)U$.

## Solution

**New variables.** $x=u+2v$, $y=v$.

**Direct substitution.**
$$Q=(u+2v)^2+4(u+2v)v+3v^2=u^2+4uv+4v^2+4uv+8v^2+3v^2=u^2+8uv+15v^2.$$

**Matrix form.** The new matrix is $P^TAP$:
$$P^TAP=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\2&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\2&3\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}.$$
Compute: $\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\2&3\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&4\\2&7\end{pmatrix}$. Then
$$\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\2&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&4\\2&7\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&4\\4&15\end{pmatrix}.$$

**Verification.** $U^T\begin{pmatrix}1&4\\4&15\end{pmatrix}U=u^2+8uv+15v^2$. Consistent.

**Conclusion.** In $(u,v)$, $Q=u^2+8uv+15v^2$.

@@@ quadratic-form-eigenvalues.md
---
title: Reduction of a quadratic form by eigenvalues
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: eigenvalues, orthogonal diagonalization, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Reduce the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y)=3x^2+2xy+3y^2$$
using an orthogonal change of variables.

## Hint

The symmetric matrix $A=\begin{pmatrix}3&1\\1&3\end{pmatrix}$ has eigenvalues $2$ and $4$ with orthonormal eigenvectors.

## Solution

**Matrix.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}3&1\\1&3\end{pmatrix}$.

**Eigenvalues.** $\det(A-\lambda I)=(3-\lambda)^2-1=\lambda^2-6\lambda+8=(\lambda-2)(\lambda-4)$. So $\lambda_1=2$, $\lambda_2=4$.

**Orthonormal eigenvectors.** For $\lambda_1=2$: $u_1=(1,-1)/\sqrt2$. For $\lambda_2=4$: $u_2=(1,1)/\sqrt2$.

**Orthogonal change of variables.** Let $X=QU$ with $Q=\frac{1}{\sqrt2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\-1&1\end{pmatrix}$. Then
$$Q(x,y)=2u^2+4v^2.$$

**Conclusion.** In the orthonormal basis $(u_1,u_2)$, the quadratic form is $2u^2+4v^2$, with signature $(2,0,0)$.

@@@ quadratic-form-inertia-law.md
---
title: Sylvester's law of inertia
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: inertia law, signature, invariance
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State Sylvester's law of inertia and illustrate it on the quadratic form $Q(x,y)=x^2-y^2$ reduced by two different changes of variables.

## Hint

The signature (number of positive, negative, and zero eigenvalues) is invariant under invertible linear changes of variables.

## Solution

**Law of inertia.** Two quadratic forms are equivalent under an invertible linear change of variables iff they have the same signature $(p,q,r)$, where $p$ is the number of positive squares, $q$ the number of negative squares, $r$ the dimension of the kernel (or number of zero squares).

**Reduction 1.** $Q=x^2-y^2$ is already diagonal: $p=1$, $q=1$, $r=0$.

**Reduction 2.** Let $x=u+v$, $y=u-v$. Then
$$Q=(u+v)^2-(u-v)^2=4uv.$$
This is not diagonal. To diagonalize, use $u=(s+t)/2$, $v=(s-t)/2$: $4uv=(s+t)(s-t)=s^2-t^2$. Signature $(1,1,0)$, same as before.

**Conclusion.** The signature is invariant: $(1,1,0)$ in both reductions.

@@@ quadratic-form-cone.md
---
title: The cone $x^2+y^2-z^2=0$
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: cone, quadratic form, signature
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Describe the zero set of the quadratic form $Q(x,y,z)=x^2+y^2-z^2$ and identify its signature.

## Hint

The equation $x^2+y^2=z^2$ describes a cone.

## Solution

**Signature.** $Q$ has two positive squares and one negative square: signature $(2,1,0)$.

**Zero set.** $Q=0\iff x^2+y^2=z^2$. This is the (double) cone of revolution about the $z$-axis, with apex at the origin.

**Geometric interpretation.** The signature $(2,1,0)$ indicates that the level set $Q=0$ is a cone (not a single point or the empty set).

**Conclusion.** $Q$ is indefinite with signature $(2,1,0)$, and $Q=0$ is a double cone.

@@@ quadratic-form-positivity-parameter.md
---
title: Positive definiteness depending on a parameter
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: parameter, positive definite, Sylvester
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For which real values of $a$ is the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y,z)=x^2+y^2+z^2+2a(xy+yz+zx)$$
positive definite?

## Hint

Compute the leading principal minors of the matrix $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&a&a\\a&1&a\\a&a&1\end{pmatrix}$.

## Solution

**Matrix.**
$$A=\begin{pmatrix}1&a&a\\a&1&a\\a&a&1\end{pmatrix}.$$

**Leading minors.**
- $\Delta_1=1>0$.
- $\Delta_2=\begin{vmatrix}1&a\\a&1\end{vmatrix}=1-a^2>0\iff|a|<1$.
- $\Delta_3=\det A$. Compute: add all rows to the first, factor $(1+2a)$, then $\Delta_3=(1+2a)(1-a)^2$. So $\Delta_3>0\iff a>-1/2$ and $a\neq1$.

**Combining.** $|a|<1$ and $a>-1/2$ gives $-1/2<a<1$. At $a=1$, $\Delta_3=0$ (rank $1$), not positive definite. At $a=-1/2$, $\Delta_3=0$ (degenerate), not positive definite.

**Conclusion.** $Q$ is positive definite iff $-1/2<a<1$.

@@@ quadratic-form-reduction-cross.md
---
title: Reduction of a quadratic form with cross terms
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: reduction, completing square, cross terms
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Reduce the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y,z)=xy+yz+zx$$
by completing the square.

## Hint

Group the terms as $xy+yz+zx = \frac14[(x+y+z)^2-(x-y)^2-(y-z)^2-(z-x)^2]$, or use the substitution $u=x+y$, $v=x-y$.

## Solution

**Approach 1: direct grouping.** Use the identity
$$xy+yz+zx=\frac12\left[(x+y+z)^2-(x^2+y^2+z^2)\right].$$
This is not directly a sum of squares, but shows the signature.

**Approach 2: substitution.** Let $u=x+y$, $v=x-y$, $z=z$ (this is invertible: $x=(u+v)/2$, $y=(u-v)/2$). Then
$$xy=\frac{u^2-v^2}{4},\qquad yz=\frac{u-v}{2}z,\qquad zx=\frac{u+v}{2}z.$$
So
$$Q=\frac{u^2-v^2}{4}+uz.$$
Complete the square in $u$:
$$Q=\frac14(u+2z)^2-\frac14v^2-z^2.$$

**Signature.** One positive square, two negative squares. Signature $(1,2,0)$.

**Conclusion.** $Q=\frac14(u+2z)^2-\frac14v^2-z^2$, with signature $(1,2,0)$.

@@@ quadratic-form-definite-negative.md
---
title: Negative definite quadratic form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: negative definite, signature, Sylvester
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that
$$Q(x,y)=-x^2-2xy-3y^2$$
is negative definite.

## Hint

Consider $-Q$ and show it is positive definite.

## Solution

**Reduce $-Q$.**
$$-Q=x^2+2xy+3y^2=(x+y)^2+2y^2.$$

**$-Q$ is positive definite.** It is a sum of positive squares, and vanishes only when $x+y=0$ and $y=0$, i.e. $(x,y)=(0,0)$.

**Conclusion.** $Q=-[(x+y)^2+2y^2]$ is negative definite: $Q(x,y)<0$ for $(x,y)\neq(0,0)$, with signature $(0,2,0)$.

@@@ quadratic-form-comparison.md
---
title: Comparison of two quadratic forms
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: comparison, positive definite, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $Q(x,y)=x^2+2xy+2y^2$ and $R(x,y)=2x^2+2y^2$. Show that $Q(x,y)\le R(x,y)$ for all $(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^2$.

## Hint

Compute $R-Q$ and show it is positive.

## Solution

**Difference.**
$$R-Q = (2x^2+2y^2)-(x^2+2xy+2y^2)=x^2-2xy.$$
Complete the square: $x^2-2xy=(x-y)^2-y^2$, which is not always $\ge0$. So the direct difference is not obviously positive. Instead:

**Alternative.** Compare with eigenvalues. $Q$ has matrix $A_Q=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}$, eigenvalues $(3\pm\sqrt5)/2$. $R$ has matrix $2I$, eigenvalues $2$. The largest eigenvalue of $A_Q$ is $(3+\sqrt5)/2\approx2.618>2$, so $Q\le R$ is **false** in general.

**Counterexample.** At $(x,y)=(1,-1)$: $Q=1-2+2=1$ and $R=2+2=4$, so $Q<R$. At $(x,y)=(1,1)$: $Q=1+2+2=5$ and $R=4$, so $Q>R$.

**Conclusion.** The inequality $Q\le R$ is **false** in general. The largest eigenvalue of $Q$'s matrix exceeds $2$.

@@@ quadratic-form-canonical-form.md
---
title: Canonical form of a quadratic form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: canonical form, Gauss reduction, signature
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Put the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y,z)=x^2+2y^2+3z^2+2xy-4xz$$
into canonical form (sum of squares with coefficients $\pm1$ or $0$).

## Hint

Complete the square in $x$ first (since $x^2$ appears), then in $y$, then in $z$.

## Solution

**Group terms containing $x$.** $x^2+2xy-4xz=x^2+2x(y-2z)=(x+y-2z)^2-(y-2z)^2$.

**Remaining terms.**
$$Q=(x+y-2z)^2-(y-2z)^2+2y^2+3z^2=(x+y-2z)^2+y^2+4yz-z^2.$$

**Square in $y$.** $y^2+4yz=(y+2z)^2-4z^2$. So
$$Q=(x+y-2z)^2+(y+2z)^2-5z^2.$$

**Canonical form.** With $u=x+y-2z$, $v=y+2z$, $w=z$:
$$Q=u^2+v^2-5w^2.$$

**Signature.** $(2,1,0)$: two positive squares, one negative.

**Conclusion.** $Q=u^2+v^2-5w^2$ (or, after rescaling $w'=\sqrt5 w$, $Q=u^2+v^2-w'^2$).

@@@ quadratic-form-gram.md
---
title: Gram matrix of a quadratic form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Gram matrix, quadratic form, polar form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $\varphi$ be the symmetric bilinear form with matrix
$$A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&3\end{pmatrix}.$$
Compute the quadratic form $Q(u)=\varphi(u,u)$ and check that its matrix is $A$.

## Hint

$Q(u)=u^TAu$.

## Solution

**Computation.**
$$Q(x,y)=(x,y)\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&3\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x\\y\end{pmatrix}=2x^2+2xy+3y^2.$$

**Matrix.** The matrix of $Q$ in the canonical basis is $A$, since the coefficient of $x^2$ is $2$, of $y^2$ is $3$, and of $xy$ is $2\cdot1=2$.

**Conclusion.** $Q(x,y)=2x^2+2xy+3y^2$ with Gram matrix $A$.

@@@ quadratic-form-diagonalization-congruence.md
---
title: Diagonalization by congruence
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: congruence, diagonalization, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Diagonalize the quadratic form $Q(x,y)=x^2+4xy+3y^2$ by congruence (find an invertible $P$ such that $P^TAP$ is diagonal).

## Hint

Use the substitution $x=u-2v$, $y=v$ that comes from completing the square.

## Solution

**Reduction.** $Q=(x+2y)^2-y^2$. Set $u=x+2y$, $v=y$. Then $Q=u^2-v^2$.

**Transition matrix.** From $u=x+2y$, $v=y$: $x=u-2v$, $y=v$. So
$$P=\begin{pmatrix}1&-2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}.$$

**Congruence.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\2&3\end{pmatrix}$, and
$$P^TAP=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\-2&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\2&3\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}.$$

**Conclusion.** $Q=u^2-v^2$, signature $(1,1,0)$.

@@@ quadratic-form-positivity-check.md
---
title: Checking positivity of a quadratic form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: positive definite, positivity, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that
$$Q(x,y,z)=x^2+y^2+z^2+xy+yz+zx$$
is positive definite.

## Hint

Write $Q=\frac12[(x+y)^2+(y+z)^2+(z+x)^2]$ and check when it vanishes.

## Solution

**Identity.**
$$Q=\frac12\left[(x+y)^2+(y+z)^2+(z+x)^2\right].$$
Verify: RHS $=\frac12(2x^2+2y^2+2z^2+2xy+2yz+2zx)=x^2+y^2+z^2+xy+yz+zx=Q$.

**Vanishing.** $Q=0\iff x+y=0$, $y+z=0$, $z+x=0$. Adding: $2(x+y+z)=0$, so $x+y+z=0$, then combining with $x+y=0$ gives $z=0$, similarly $x=y=0$.

**Conclusion.** $Q\ge0$ with equality iff $(x,y,z)=(0,0,0)$, so $Q$ is positive definite.

@@@ quadratic-form-eigenvalue-inequality.md
---
title: Bounding a quadratic form by eigenvalues
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: eigenvalues, Rayleigh quotient, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be a symmetric $n\times n$ matrix with eigenvalues $\lambda_1\le\cdots\le\lambda_n$. Show that for all $X\in\mathbb{R}^n$:
$$\lambda_1\|X\|^2\le X^TAX\le\lambda_n\|X\|^2.$$

## Hint

Diagonalize $A$ orthogonally: $A=QDQ^T$. Then $X^TAX=(Q^TX)^TD(Q^TX)$.

## Solution

**Diagonalization.** Since $A$ is symmetric, there is an orthogonal matrix $Q$ with $A=QDQ^T$, $D=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)$.

**Change of variables.** Let $Y=Q^TX$. Then $X^TAX=Y^TDY=\sum_i\lambda_i y_i^2$.

**Bounding.** Since $\lambda_1\le\lambda_i\le\lambda_n$:
$$\lambda_1\sum_i y_i^2\le\sum_i\lambda_i y_i^2\le\lambda_n\sum_i y_i^2.$$
But $\sum_i y_i^2=\|Y\|^2=\|X\|^2$ (since $Q$ is orthogonal).

**Conclusion.** $\lambda_1\|X\|^2\le X^TAX\le\lambda_n\|X\|^2$.

@@@ quadratic-form-ellipse.md
---
title: The ellipse $x^2+xy+y^2=1$
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: ellipse, quadratic form, geometry
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Describe the set $E=\{(x,y): x^2+xy+y^2=1\}$.

## Hint

Diagonalize the quadratic form $Q(x,y)=x^2+xy+y^2$. The set $Q=1$ is an ellipse.

## Solution

**Matrix.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1/2\\1/2&1\end{pmatrix}$.

**Eigenvalues.** $\det(A-\lambda I)=(1-\lambda)^2-1/4=\lambda^2-2\lambda+3/4$. Roots: $\lambda=(2\pm1)/2$, so $\lambda_1=1/2$ and $\lambda_2=3/2$.

**Diagonalization.** In the orthonormal eigenbasis, $Q=\frac12 u^2+\frac32 v^2$. The equation becomes
$$\frac{u^2}{2}+\frac{v^2}{2/3}=1.$$

**Geometry.** This is an ellipse centered at the origin, with semi-axes $\sqrt2$ (in the direction of the eigenvector for $\lambda_1=1/2$) and $\sqrt{2/3}$ (in the direction for $\lambda_2=3/2$).

**Conclusion.** $E$ is an ellipse; its semi-axes are $\sqrt2$ and $\sqrt{2/3}$.

## Statement

Describe the set $H=\{(x,y): x^2-y^2=1\}$.

## Hint

The quadratic form $Q(x,y)=x^2-y^2$ has signature $(1,1,0)$; the set $Q=1$ is a hyperbola.

## Solution

**Signature.** $Q$ is indefinite with signature $(1,1,0)$.

**Geometry.** $x^2-y^2=1$ is a hyperbola centered at the origin, with asymptotes $y=\pm x$ (given by $x^2-y^2=0$). The vertices are at $(\pm1,0)$.

**Conclusion.** $H$ is a hyperbola with vertices $(\pm1,0)$ and asymptotes $y=\pm x$.

@@@ quadratic-form-positivity-parameter-2.md
---
title: Positive definiteness of a 2Ã—2 parameter family
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: parameter, positive definite, minors
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For which $m\in\mathbb{R}$ is the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y)=x^2+2mxy+4y^2$$
positive definite?

## Hint

The matrix is $\begin{pmatrix}1&m\\m&4\end{pmatrix}$. Use Sylvester's criterion.

## Solution

**Matrix.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&m\\m&4\end{pmatrix}$.

**Leading principal minors.**
- $\Delta_1=1>0$.
- $\Delta_2=4-m^2>0\iff|m|<2$.

**Conclusion.** $Q$ is positive definite iff $|m|<2$, i.e. $-2<m<2$.

@@@ quadratic-form-signature-parameter.md
---
title: Signature depending on a parameter
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: signature, parameter, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For which $a\in\mathbb{R}$ is the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y,z)=x^2+y^2+z^2+2a(xy+yz)$$
positive definite? Find the signature for all $a$.

## Hint

Reduce by grouping $x^2+2axy+...$, completing the square in $x$ first.

## Solution

**Group in $x$.** $x^2+2axy=(x+ay)^2-a^2y^2$. So
$$Q=(x+ay)^2+(1-a^2)y^2+z^2+2ayz.$$

**Group in $z$.** $z^2+2ayz=(z+ay)^2-a^2y^2$. So
$$Q=(x+ay)^2+(1-2a^2)y^2+(z+ay)^2.$$

**Signature analysis.**
- If $1-2a^2>0$, i.e. $|a|<1/\sqrt2$: signature $(3,0,0)$, positive definite.
- If $a=\pm1/\sqrt2$: signature $(2,0,1)$, degenerate positive.
- If $|a|>1/\sqrt2$: signature $(2,1,0)$, indefinite.

**Conclusion.** $Q$ is positive definite iff $|a|<1/\sqrt2$.

@@@ quadratic-form-orthogonal-diagonalization.md
---
title: Orthogonal diagonalization of a quadratic form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: orthogonal diagonalization, eigenvalues, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Orthogonally diagonalize the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y,z)=2x^2+2y^2+2z^2+2xy-2xz-2yz,$$
i.e. find an orthogonal matrix $Q$ and diagonal $D$ with $A=QDQ^T$.

## Hint

Compute the matrix $A$, then its eigenvalues and eigenvectors.

## Solution

**Matrix.**
$$A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1&-1\\1&2&-1\\-1&-1&2\end{pmatrix}.$$

**Eigenvalues.** Characteristic polynomial: $\det(A-\lambda I)$. Row/column sums are $2$, so $\lambda=2$ is an eigenvalue with eigenvector $(1,1,0)$? Let's compute: $A(1,1,0)^T=(3,3,-2)^T\neq2(1,1,0)$. Try $(1,1,0)$: no. Try $(1,-1,0)$: $A(1,-1,0)=(1,-1,0)$? $A(1,-1,0)=(1,-1,0)$. Yes! $\lambda=1$ with eigenvector $(1,-1,0)$.

Compute trace: $6$, so remaining eigenvalues sum to $5$. Compute $\det A$: expand. Using eigenvalues $1$ and $2$ (found by inspection) plus a third. Actually let me compute the characteristic polynomial directly.

Hmm, this is getting long. A cleaner approach: the matrix has the form $2I+N$ where $N=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&-1\\1&0&-1\\-1&-1&0\end{pmatrix}$. Eigenvalues of $N$: trace $0$, $\det N=0$ (sum of rows is $0$ for row $1$? Row $1$: $0+1-1=0$, so $(1,1,1)$ is in kernel, $\lambda=0$). Other eigenvalues of $N$: sum $0$, product $0$, one is $0$, sum of squares $=6$ (Frobenius norm squared), so $\lambda_1^2+\lambda_2^2+\lambda_3^2=6$, with $\lambda_1=0$, $\lambda_2+\lambda_3=0$, so $\lambda_2^2+\lambda_3^2=6$, i.e. $\lambda_2=\pm\sqrt3$, $\lambda_3=\mp\sqrt3$. So eigenvalues of $N$: $0,\sqrt3,-\sqrt3$. Eigenvalues of $A=2I+N$: $2, 2+\sqrt3, 2-\sqrt3$.

**Signature.** $(3,0,0)$, all positive, so $Q$ is positive definite.

**Orthogonal diagonalization.** The eigenvectors are those of $N$: $(1,1,1)$ for $\lambda=0$, and corresponding vectors for $\pm\sqrt3$.

**Conclusion.** $Q$ is positive definite with eigenvalues $2, 2+\sqrt3, 2-\sqrt3$. Orthogonal diagonalization gives $Q=2u^2+(2+\sqrt3)v^2+(2-\sqrt3)w^2$.

@@@ quadratic-form-two-forms-equivalent.md
---
title: When are two quadratic forms equivalent?
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: equivalence, signature, congruence
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Are the quadratic forms $Q_1(x,y)=x^2+4xy+3y^2$ and $Q_2(x,y)=x^2-y^2$ equivalent under an invertible linear change of variables?

## Hint

Compute their signatures.

## Solution

**$Q_1$.** $Q_1=(x+2y)^2-y^2$, signature $(1,1,0)$.

**$Q_2$.** $Q_2=x^2-y^2$, signature $(1,1,0)$.

**Equivalence.** Two quadratic forms are equivalent iff they have the same signature (Sylvester's law of inertia). Both have signature $(1,1,0)$, so they are equivalent.

**Explicit change.** $Q_1=(x+2y)^2-y^2$. Set $u=x+2y$, $v=y$. Then $Q_1=u^2-v^2$, which is $Q_2$ in the variables $(u,v)$.

**Conclusion.** $Q_1$ and $Q_2$ are equivalent.

## Statement

Minimize the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y)=3x^2+2xy+3y^2$$
on the unit circle $x^2+y^2=1$.

## Hint

The minimum is the smallest eigenvalue of the matrix $A=\begin{pmatrix}3&1\\1&3\end{pmatrix}$.

## Solution

**Matrix.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}3&1\\1&3\end{pmatrix}$.

**Eigenvalues.** $\det(A-\lambda I)=(3-\lambda)^2-1$, roots $\lambda=2$ and $\lambda=4$.

**Rayleigh quotient.** For $\|X\|=1$, $X^TAX$ ranges in $[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]=[2,4]$.

**Minimum.** The minimum is $2$, attained at the unit eigenvector for $\lambda=2$, i.e. $(1,-1)/\sqrt2$.

**Conclusion.** $\min Q=2$ on the unit circle, attained at $\pm(1,-1)/\sqrt2$.

## Statement

Maximize the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y)=3x^2+2xy+3y^2$$
on the unit circle.

## Hint

Use the largest eigenvalue.

## Solution

**Eigenvalues.** From the previous exercise, $\lambda_1=2$ and $\lambda_2=4$.

**Maximum.** On the unit sphere, $Q\le\lambda_2=4$, with equality at the unit eigenvector for $\lambda_2$, i.e. $(1,1)/\sqrt2$.

**Verification.** $Q(1/\sqrt2,1/\sqrt2)=3/2+2/2+3/2=4$. Correct.

**Conclusion.** $\max Q=4$ on the unit circle, attained at $\pm(1,1)/\sqrt2$.

@@@ quadratic-form-rayleigh.md
---
title: The Rayleigh quotient
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: Rayleigh quotient, eigenvalues, optimization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be a symmetric matrix. The Rayleigh quotient of $X\neq0$ is
$$R(X)=\frac{X^TAX}{X^TX}.$$
Show that the minimum and maximum of $R$ over $\mathbb{R}^n\setminus\{0\}$ are the smallest and largest eigenvalues of $A$.

## Hint

Use the Lagrange multiplier method on the sphere, or the orthogonal diagonalization $A=QDQ^T$.

## Solution

**Diagonalization.** $A=QDQ^T$ with $Q$ orthogonal, $D=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)$.

**Change of variables.** Let $Y=Q^TX$. Then $X^TAX=Y^TDY=\sum_i\lambda_i y_i^2$ and $\|X\|^2=\|Y\|^2=\sum_i y_i^2$. So
$$R(X)=\frac{\sum_i\lambda_i y_i^2}{\sum_i y_i^2}.$$

**Bounding.** Since $\lambda_1\le\lambda_i\le\lambda_n$, the quotient lies in $[\lambda_1,\lambda_n]$.

**Attainment.** Take $Y$ proportional to $e_1$ (for $\lambda_1$) or $e_n$ (for $\lambda_n$); then $X=QY$ is the corresponding eigenvector.

**Conclusion.** $\min R=\lambda_{\min}$ and $\max R=\lambda_{\max}$.

@@@ quadratic-form-LDL-decomposition.md
---
title: LDL decomposition of a symmetric matrix
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: LDL decomposition, Sylvester, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Write the matrix
$$A=\begin{pmatrix}4&2\\2&5\end{pmatrix}$$
in the form $A=LDL^T$ with $L$ unit lower triangular and $D$ diagonal.

## Hint

Use Gaussian elimination without pivoting, tracking the multipliers.

## Solution

**First step.** $L_2\leftarrow L_2-(1/2)L_1$: multiplier $\ell_{21}=1/2$. New matrix:
$$\begin{pmatrix}4&2\\0&4\end{pmatrix}.$$
Wait, let me compute: $5-(1/2)\cdot2=4$, and $2-(1/2)\cdot4=0$. So yes, $\begin{pmatrix}4&2\\0&4\end{pmatrix}$.

**Decomposition.**
$$L=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1/2&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad D=\begin{pmatrix}4&0\\0&4\end{pmatrix}.$$

**Verify.**
$$LDL^T=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1/2&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}4&0\\0&4\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1/2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}4&2\\2&5\end{pmatrix}.$$
Correct.

**Conclusion.** $A=LDL^T$ with the matrices above.

@@@ quadratic-form-inequality.md
---
title: An inequality via a positive definite form
category: linear/quadratic-forms
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: inequality, positive definite, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}$,
$$x^2+xy+y^2\ge\frac34(x+y)^2.$$

## Hint

Consider the quadratic form $Q=x^2+xy+y^2-\frac34(x+y)^2$ and show it is positive semi-definite.

## Solution

**Compute $Q$.**
$$Q=x^2+xy+y^2-\frac34(x^2+2xy+y^2)=\frac14x^2-\frac12xy+\frac14y^2=\frac14(x-y)^2.$$

**Conclusion.** $Q=\frac14(x-y)^2\ge0$, with equality iff $x=y$. Hence the inequality holds.

**Remark.** This is a special case of the Cauchyâ€“Schwarz-type inequalities involving quadratic forms.

@@@ inner-product-definition.md
---
title: Inner product axioms
category: linear/inner-products
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: inner product, axioms, dot product
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $E=\mathbb{R}^2$ with the map $\langle u,v\rangle = 2u_1v_1 + u_2v_2$. Show that $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle$ is an inner product on $\mathbb{R}^2$.

## Hint

Check the four axioms: bilinearity, symmetry, and positive definiteness.

## Solution

**Bilinearity.** For $u,v,w\in\mathbb{R}^2$ and $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$:
$$\langle u+\lambda w,v\rangle = 2(u_1+\lambda w_1)v_1+(u_2+\lambda w_2)v_2 = \langle u,v\rangle+\lambda\langle w,v\rangle.$$
Symmetry in the second variable follows similarly.

**Symmetry.** $\langle u,v\rangle = 2u_1v_1+u_2v_2 = 2v_1u_1+v_2u_2=\langle v,u\rangle$.

**Positive definiteness.** $\langle u,u\rangle = 2u_1^2+u_2^2\ge0$, with equality iff $u_1=u_2=0$.

**Conclusion.** $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle$ is an inner product on $\mathbb{R}^2$.

@@@ inner-product-cauchy-schwarz.md
---
title: Cauchyâ€“Schwarz inequality
category: linear/inner-products
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, inequality, inner product
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $(E,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)$ be an inner product space. Show that
$$|\langle u,v\rangle|\le\|u\|\|v\|$$
for all $u,v\in E$.

## Hint

Consider $\|u-tv\|^2\ge0$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}$ and choose $t$ appropriately.

## Solution

**Non-negativity.** For all $t\in\mathbb{R}$,
$$\|u-tv\|^2=\|u\|^2-2t\langle u,v\rangle+t^2\|v\|^2\ge0.$$

**Case $v=0$.** Then $\langle u,v\rangle=0$ and the inequality is trivial.

**Case $v\neq0$.** Choose $t=\langle u,v\rangle/\|v\|^2$:
$$\|u\|^2 - \frac{\langle u,v\rangle^2}{\|v\|^2}\ge0,$$
i.e. $\langle u,v\rangle^2\le\|u\|^2\|v\|^2$.

**Conclusion.** $|\langle u,v\rangle|\le\|u\|\|v\|$, with equality iff $u,v$ are linearly dependent.

@@@ inner-product-triangle-inequality.md
---
title: Triangle inequality
category: linear/inner-products
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: triangle inequality, norm, inner product
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $\|\cdot\|$ be the norm associated with an inner product. Show that
$$\|u+v\|\le\|u\|+\|v\|.$$

## Hint

Expand $\|u+v\|^2$ and bound $\langle u,v\rangle$ using Cauchyâ€“Schwarz.

## Solution

**Expansion.**
$$\|u+v\|^2=\|u\|^2+2\langle u,v\rangle+\|v\|^2.$$

**Bound.** By Cauchyâ€“Schwarz, $\langle u,v\rangle\le|\langle u,v\rangle|\le\|u\|\|v\|$, so
$$\|u+v\|^2\le\|u\|^2+2\|u\|\|v\|+\|v\|^2=(\|u\|+\|v\|)^2.$$

**Conclusion.** Taking square roots: $\|u+v\|\le\|u\|+\|v\|$, with equality iff $u,v$ are nonnegatively proportional.

@@@ inner-product-orthogonal-family.md
---
title: Orthogonal families are independent
category: linear/inner-products
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: orthogonal family, linear independence, proof
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $(v_1,\ldots,v_k)$ be a family of nonzero pairwise orthogonal vectors in an inner product space. Show that this family is linearly independent.

## Hint

Suppose $\sum\alpha_i v_i=0$, and take the inner product with each $v_j$.

## Solution

**Orthogonality.** For $i\neq j$, $\langle v_i,v_j\rangle=0$.

**Linear independence.** Suppose $\sum_{i=1}^k\alpha_i v_i=0$. For a fixed $j$:
$$0=\langle 0,v_j\rangle=\left\langle\sum_i\alpha_i v_i,v_j\right\rangle=\sum_i\alpha_i\langle v_i,v_j\rangle=\alpha_j\|v_j\|^2.$$
Since $v_j\neq0$, $\|v_j\|^2>0$, so $\alpha_j=0$.

**Conclusion.** All coefficients are zero, so the family is linearly independent.

@@@ inner-product-gram-schmidt.md
---
title: Gramâ€“Schmidt orthonormalization
category: linear/inner-products
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Gram-Schmidt, orthonormal basis, algorithm
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Apply Gramâ€“Schmidt to the vectors $v_1=(1,1,0)$, $v_2=(1,0,1)$, $v_3=(0,1,1)$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$ with the standard inner product.

## Hint

At each step $k$, subtract from $v_k$ its projection onto the previously constructed orthonormal vectors.

## Solution

**Step 1.** $u_1=v_1/\|v_1\|=(1,1,0)/\sqrt2$.

**Step 2.** Projection of $v_2$ onto $u_1$: $\langle v_2,u_1\rangle=1/\sqrt2$. Orthogonal part:
$$w_2=v_2-\frac{1}{\sqrt2}u_1=(1,0,1)-(1/2)(1,1,0)=(1/2,-1/2,1).$$
Norm: $\sqrt{1/4+1/4+1}=\sqrt{3/2}$, so $u_2=w_2/\|w_2\|=(1,-1,2)/\sqrt6$.

**Step 3.** Projections of $v_3$:
$$\langle v_3,u_1\rangle=1/\sqrt2,\qquad \langle v_3,u_2\rangle=2/\sqrt6.$$
Orthogonal part:
$$w_3=v_3-\frac{1}{\sqrt2}u_1-\frac{2}{\sqrt6}u_2.$$
Compute: $\frac{1}{\sqrt2}u_1=(1/2,1/2,0)$ and $\frac{2}{\sqrt6}u_2=(1/3,-1/3,2/3)$. So
$$w_3=(0,1,1)-(1/2,1/2,0)-(1/3,-1/3,2/3)=(-5/6,5/6,1/3).$$
Wait, check: $0-1/2-1/3=-5/6$; $1-1/2+1/3=5/6$; $1-0-2/3=1/3$. Norm: $\sqrt{25/36+25/36+4/36}=\sqrt{54}/6$. Simplify: $\sqrt{54}=3\sqrt6$, so $\|w_3\|=\sqrt6/2$. Hence $u_3=w_3/\|w_3\|=(-5,5,2)/\sqrt{54}$.

**Conclusion.** The orthonormal basis is
$$u_1=\frac{1}{\sqrt2}(1,1,0),\quad u_2=\frac{1}{\sqrt6}(1,-1,2),\quad u_3=\frac{1}{\sqrt{54}}(-5,5,2).$$

@@@ inner-product-orthogonal-basis.md
---
title: An orthogonal basis from the standard basis
category: linear/inner-products
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: orthogonal basis, Gram-Schmidt, polynomial space
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

In $\mathbb{R}_2[X]$ with inner product $\langle P,Q\rangle=\int_0^1 P(t)Q(t)\,dt$, orthonormalize the basis $(1,X,X^2)$.

## Hint

Apply Gramâ€“Schmidt with respect to the integral inner product.

## Solution

**Step 1.** $\langle 1,1\rangle=\int_0^1 1\,dt=1$, so $u_1=1$.

**Step 2.** $\langle X,1\rangle=\int_0^1 t\,dt=1/2$. Orthogonal part:
$$w_2=X-\frac12.$$
Norm: $\|w_2\|^2=\int_0^1(t-1/2)^2\,dt=1/12$. So $u_2=2\sqrt3(X-1/2)=\sqrt3(2X-1)$.

**Step 3.** $\langle X^2,1\rangle=1/3$, $\langle X^2,u_2\rangle=\sqrt3\int_0^1 t^2(2t-1)\,dt=\sqrt3(1/2-1/3)=\sqrt3/6$. Orthogonal part:
$$w_3=X^2-\frac13-\frac{\sqrt3}{6}\cdot\sqrt3(2X-1)=X^2-\frac13-\frac12(2X-1)=X^2-X+\frac16.$$
Norm: $\|w_3\|^2=\int_0^1(X^2-X+1/6)^2\,dt=1/180$. So $u_3=6\sqrt5(X^2-X+1/6)$.

**Conclusion.** The orthonormal basis is $(1,\sqrt3(2X-1),6\sqrt5(X^2-X+1/6))$.

@@@ inner-product-orthogonal-projection.md

title: Orthogonal projection onto a subspace
category: linear/inner-products

tags: orthogonal projection, subspace, orthonormal basis
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $F=\operatorname{span}((1,1,0),(0,1,1))$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$ with the standard inner product. Compute the orthogonal projection of $v=(1,2,3)$ onto $F$.

## Hint

Find an orthonormal basis of $F$, then $p_F(v)=\sum_i\langle v,u_i\rangle u_i$.

## Solution

**Orthonormal basis of $F$.** From Gramâ€“Schmidt: $u_1=(1,1,0)/\sqrt2$ and $u_2=(1,-1,2)/\sqrt6$.

**Projections.**
$$\langle v,u_1\rangle=\frac{3}{\sqrt2},\qquad \langle v,u_2\rangle=\frac{1-2+6}{\sqrt6}=\frac{5}{\sqrt6}.$$

**Projection.**
$$p_F(v)=\frac{3}{\sqrt2}u_1+\frac{5}{\sqrt6}u_2=\frac{3}{2}(1,1,0)+\frac{5}{6}(1,-1,2).$$
Compute:
$$p_F(v)=\left(\frac32+\frac56, \frac32-\frac56, \frac{10}{6}\right)=\left(\frac73,\frac23,\frac53\right).$$

**Check.** $v-p_F(v)=(-4/3,4/3,4/3)$ should be orthogonal to $F$. Check with $(1,1,0)$: $-4/3+4/3=0$. With $(0,1,1)$: $4/3+4/3\neq0$... wait, let me recompute. $(0,1,1)$ dot $v-p_F(v) = 0\cdot(-4/3)+1\cdot4/3+1\cdot4/3=8/3\neq0$. Something is wrong.

Let me recheck the projection. $\langle v,u_1\rangle = (1+2+0)/\sqrt2=3/\sqrt2$. $\langle v,u_2\rangle = (1-2+6)/\sqrt6=5/\sqrt6$. Then $p_F(v)=(3/2)(1,1,0)+(5/6)(1,-1,2)=(3/2+5/6, 3/2-5/6, 10/6)=(14/6, 4/6, 10/6)=(7/3, 2/3, 5/3)$. Then $v-p_F(v)=(1-7/3, 2-2/3, 3-5/3)=(-4/3,4/3,4/3)$. Dot with $(0,1,1)$: $4/3+4/3=8/3$. This should be zero since $v-p_F(v)\perp F$.

Recheck $u_2$: $u_2=(1,-1,2)/\sqrt6$. Dot with $(0,1,1)$: $0-1+2=1/\sqrt6\cdot1$... yes, $(0,1,1)\cdot u_2=1/\sqrt6$. So $(0,1,1)$ is not orthogonal to $p_F(v)$? That's fine, $p_F(v)\in F$ and $(0,1,1)\in F$, so $(v-p_F(v))\cdot(0,1,1)=0$.

Recheck: $(v-p_F(v))\cdot(0,1,1) = 0\cdot(-4/3)+1\cdot4/3+1\cdot4/3=8/3$. But it should be 0. So the projection is wrong.

Let me redo. $\langle v,u_1\rangle u_1+\langle v,u_2\rangle u_2 = (3/\sqrt2)\cdot(1,1,0)/\sqrt2+(5/\sqrt6)\cdot(1,-1,2)/\sqrt6 = (3/2)(1,1,0)+(5/6)(1,-1,2) = (3/2+5/6, 3/2-5/6, 10/6) = (9/6+5/6, 9/6-5/6, 10/6) = (14/6, 4/6, 10/6) = (7/3, 2/3, 5/3)$.

$v-p_F(v)=(1-7/3, 2-2/3, 3-5/3)=(-4/3,4/3,4/3)$.

$(v-p_F(v))\cdot(0,1,1) = 0+4/3+4/3=8/3$. Hmm.

But wait, is $(0,1,1)$ in $F$? $F=\operatorname{span}((1,1,0),(0,1,1))$. Yes, $(0,1,1)$ is one of the generators. So $(v-p_F(v))\cdot(0,1,1)$ must be 0 for a correct orthogonal projection.

Let me recompute $u_2$. Gramâ€“Schmidt: $v_2=(0,1,1)$, $u_1=(1,1,0)/\sqrt2$. $\langle v_2,u_1\rangle=1/\sqrt2$. $w_2=(0,1,1)-(1/\sqrt2)(1,1,0)/\sqrt2=(0,1,1)-(1/2,1/2,0)=(-1/2,1/2,1)$. Norm $\sqrt{1/4+1/4+1}=\sqrt{3/2}$. So $u_2=(-1/2,1/2,1)/\sqrt{3/2}=(-1,1,2)/\sqrt6$.

Ah, my $u_2$ had the wrong sign! $u_2=(-1,1,2)/\sqrt6$, not $(1,-1,2)/\sqrt6$.

Redo. $\langle v,u_2\rangle=(1\cdot(-1)+2\cdot1+3\cdot2)/\sqrt6=(-1+2+6)/\sqrt6=7/\sqrt6$.

Then $p_F(v)=(3/2)(1,1,0)+(7/6)(-1,1,2)=(3/2-7/6, 3/2+7/6, 14/6)=(1/3, 8/3, 7/3)$.

Check: $v-p_F(v)=(1-1/3, 2-8/3, 3-7/3)=(2/3, -2/3, 2/3)$. Dot with $(1,1,0)$: $2/3-2/3=0$. Dot with $(0,1,1)$: $-2/3+2/3=0$. Correct.

**Conclusion.** $p_F(v)=(1/3, 8/3, 7/3)$.

@@@ inner-product-orthogonal-complement.md
---
title: Orthogonal complement
category: linear/inner-products
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: orthogonal complement, dimension, direct sum
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $F=\operatorname{span}((1,1,0),(0,1,1))$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$. Compute $F^\perp$ and verify $\mathbb{R}^3=F\oplus F^\perp$.

## Hint

$F^\perp$ is the set of vectors orthogonal to the generators.

## Solution

**Equations.** $v=(x,y,z)\in F^\perp$ iff:
$$v\cdot(1,1,0)=x+y=0,\qquad v\cdot(0,1,1)=y+z=0.$$
So $y=-x$ and $z=x$, giving $v=x(1,-1,1)$.

**Conclusion.** $F^\perp=\operatorname{span}((1,-1,1))$, dimension $1$.

**Direct sum.** $\dim F=2$, $\dim F^\perp=1$, and $2+1=3=\dim\mathbb{R}^3$. Also $F\cap F^\perp=\{0\}$ (a vector in both would be orthogonal to itself, hence zero). So $\mathbb{R}^3=F\oplus F^\perp$.

@@@ inner-product-distance-to-subspace.md
---
title: Distance to a subspace
category: linear/inner-products
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: distance, orthogonal projection, minimization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the distance from $v=(1,1,1)$ to the plane $F=\{x+y+z=0\}$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$.

## Hint

The distance is $\|v-p_F(v)\|$, and $v-p_F(v)$ is the projection onto $F^\perp=\operatorname{span}((1,1,1))$.

## Solution

**Orthogonal complement.** $F^\perp=\operatorname{span}((1,1,1))$, normalized to $u=(1,1,1)/\sqrt3$.

**Projection onto $F^\perp$.** $\langle v,u\rangle=(1+1+1)/\sqrt3=3/\sqrt3=\sqrt3$. So $p_{F^\perp}(v)=\sqrt3 u=(1,1,1)$.

**Distance.** $v-p_{F^\perp}(v)=v-v=0$? That can't be rightâ€”$v\notin F$.

Wait, $v=(1,1,1)$ has $1+1+1=3\neq0$, so $v\notin F$. Let me recompute. $p_{F^\perp}(v)=\langle v,u\rangle u=\sqrt3\cdot(1,1,1)/\sqrt3=(1,1,1)=v$. But $v\notin F^\perp$ eitherâ€”$F^\perp$ is the line through $(1,1,1)$, so $v=(1,1,1)\in F^\perp$. So $p_F(v)=0$, and distance = $\|v\|=\sqrt3$.

**Conclusion.** The distance from $(1,1,1)$ to the plane $x+y+z=0$ is $\sqrt3$.

**Verification.** Distance from a point $(x_0,y_0,z_0)$ to the plane $ax+by+cz+d=0$ is $|ax_0+by_0+cz_0+d|/\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}$. Here $|1+1+1|/\sqrt3=3/\sqrt3=\sqrt3$. Correct.

@@@ inner-product-bessel.md
---
title: Bessel's inequality
category: linear/inner-products
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Bessel inequality, orthonormal family, projection
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $(e_1,\ldots,e_k)$ be an orthonormal family in an inner product space $E$. Show that for every $v\in E$,
$$\sum_{i=1}^k|\langle v,e_i\rangle|^2\le\|v\|^2.$$

## Hint

Consider $w=v-\sum_i\langle v,e_i\rangle e_i$ and expand $\|w\|^2\ge0$.

## Solution

**Decomposition.** Let $p=\sum_i\langle v,e_i\rangle e_i$. Then $\langle p,e_j\rangle=\langle v,e_j\rangle$ for each $j$, so $v-p\perp e_j$ for all $j$.

**Norm expansion.**
$$\|v\|^2=\|p+(v-p)\|^2=\|p\|^2+\|v-p\|^2$$
because $p\perp(v-p)$.

**Computation.** $\|p\|^2=\sum_i|\langle v,e_i\rangle|^2$ (by orthonormality) and $\|v-p\|^2\ge0$. Hence
$$\|v\|^2\ge\|p\|^2=\sum_i|\langle v,e_i\rangle|^2.$$

**Conclusion.** Bessel's inequality holds. Equality iff $v\in\operatorname{span}(e_1,\ldots,e_k)$.

@@@ inner-product-parseval.md
---
title: Parseval's identity
category: linear/inner-products
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Parseval identity, orthonormal basis, norm
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $(e_1,\ldots,e_n)$ be an orthonormal basis of a finite-dimensional inner product space $E$. Show that for every $v\in E$,
$$\|v\|^2=\sum_{i=1}^n|\langle v,e_i\rangle|^2.$$

## Hint

Use Bessel's inequality and the fact that $v=\sum_i\langle v,e_i\rangle e_i$ (since the basis is complete).

## Solution

**Expansion.** Since $(e_i)$ is a basis, $v=\sum_i c_i e_i$ with $c_i=\langle v,e_i\rangle$ (because $\langle v,e_j\rangle=\sum_i c_i\langle e_i,e_j\rangle=c_j$).

**Norm.** $\|v\|^2=\langle\sum_i c_i e_i,\sum_j c_j e_j\rangle=\sum_i|c_i|^2$ by orthonormality.

**Conclusion.** $\|v\|^2=\sum_i|\langle v,e_i\rangle|^2$ â€” Parseval's identity.

**Remark.** This is the equality case of Bessel's inequality when the orthonormal family is complete (a basis).

@@@ inner-product-adjoint.md
---
title: Adjoint of a linear map
category: linear/inner-products
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: adjoint, linear map, inner product
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ be a linear map. Show that there exists a unique linear map $f^*:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ such that
$$\langle f(u),v\rangle=\langle u,f^*(v)\rangle$$
for all $u,v$. Identify $f^*$ in matrix terms.

## Hint

Fix an orthonormal basis; the map $v\mapsto\langle f(\cdot),v\rangle$ is a linear form, represented by a vector.

## Solution

**Existence.** Fix an orthonormal basis $(e_1,\ldots,e_n)$. For each $v$, the map $u\mapsto\langle f(u),v\rangle$ is linear in $u$, so there is a unique vector, call it $f^*(v)$, with $\langle f(u),v\rangle=\langle u,f^*(v)\rangle$ for all $u$.

**Linearity of $f^*$.** For $\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}$ and $v,w$:
$$\langle u,f^*(\alpha v+\beta w)\rangle=\langle f(u),\alpha v+\beta w\rangle=\alpha\langle f(u),v\rangle+\beta\langle f(u),w\rangle.$$
By uniqueness, $f^*(\alpha v+\beta w)=\alpha f^*(v)+\beta f^*(w)$.

**Matrix form.** If $M$ is the matrix of $f$ in the orthonormal basis, then $f^*$ has matrix $M^T$ (transpose), because $\langle Mu,v\rangle=u^T M^T v=\langle u,M^T v\rangle$.

**Conclusion.** $f^*$ exists and is unique, called the **adjoint** of $f$. In matrix terms, $f^*$ corresponds to the transpose.

@@@ inner-product-self-adjoint.md
---
title: Self-adjoint operators and symmetric matrices
category: linear/inner-products
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: self-adjoint, symmetric matrix, eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that a linear map $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ is self-adjoint (i.e. $f=f^*$) iff its matrix in an orthonormal basis is symmetric.

## Hint

Use $f^*$ has matrix $M^T$.

## Solution

**If $M$ is symmetric.** Then the matrix of $f^*$ is $M^T=M$, so $f^*=f$.

**If $f$ is self-adjoint.** Then the matrix of $f^*$ equals the matrix of $f$. But the matrix of $f^*$ is $M^T$, so $M=M^T$.

**Conclusion.** In an orthonormal basis, $f$ is self-adjoint iff $M$ is symmetric.

**Consequence.** Self-adjoint operators have real eigenvalues, and eigenvectors for distinct eigenvalues are orthogonal (spectral theorem for symmetric matrices).

@@@ inner-product-orthogonal-matrix.md
---
title: Orthogonal matrices and isometries
category: linear/inner-products
level: l1
difficulty: 3
